# Daily Summary: 2025-06-22

### Cost-Aware Routing for Efficient Text-To-Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14753v1)
- **Authors**: Qinchan, Li, Kenneth Chen, Changyue, Su, Wittawat Jitkrittum, Qi Sun, Patsorn Sangkloy
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models are well known for their ability to generate a high-fidelity image for an input prompt through an iterative denoising process. Unfortunately, the high fidelity also comes at a high computational cost due the inherently sequential generative process. In this work, we seek to optimally balance quality and computational cost, and propose a framework to allow the amount of computation to vary for each prompt, depending on its complexity. Each prompt is automatically routed to the most appropriate text-to-image generation function, which may correspond to a distinct number of denoising steps of a diffusion model, or a disparate, independent text-to-image model. Unlike uniform cost reduction techniques (e.g., distillation, model quantization), our approach achieves the optimal trade-off by learning to reserve expensive choices (e.g., 100+ denoising steps) only for a few complex prompts, and employ more economical choices (e.g., small distilled model) for less sophisticated prompts. We empirically demonstrate on COCO and DiffusionDB that by learning to route to nine already-trained text-to-image models, our approach is able to deliver an average quality that is higher than that achievable by any of these models alone.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Cost-Aware Routing for Efficient Text-To-Image Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CATImage, a framework designed to improve the efficiency of text-to-image generation by adaptively allocating computational resources based on the complexity of the input prompt.  Instead of applying a uniform level of optimization (like distillation or quantization) across all prompts, CATImage learns to route each prompt to the most appropriate generation function. These functions can differ in the number of denoising steps used in diffusion models, or even correspond to entirely different pre-trained generative models.  The framework is formulated as a constrained optimization problem, balancing image quality against computational cost. The authors demonstrate that, by learning to route to a diverse set of pre-trained models, their approach can achieve higher average quality at a lower average cost compared to using any single model in isolation.  The approach is evaluated on COCO and DiffusionDB datasets, using different quality metrics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of adaptively allocating resources based on input complexity is intuitively appealing and has been explored in other contexts, such as adaptive computation time in recurrent neural networks or conditional computation. The novelty lies in applying this principle specifically to text-to-image generation with diffusion models and other generative architectures.  The formulation as a constrained optimization problem is standard, but the application and analysis of the theoretically optimal routing rule is a useful contribution. There's a certain amount of novelty in framing this problem as "model routing" given its inspiration from the Natural Language Processing world.

*   **Significance:** The problem being addressed is highly significant. Diffusion models, while powerful, are computationally expensive, limiting their practical deployment. Reducing the cost of generation without sacrificing quality is crucial for wider adoption, particularly in resource-constrained environments (e.g., mobile devices). The experimental results demonstrate promising gains in quality-cost trade-offs.  The demonstration that prompts with varying complexity can be handled with varying levels of computation leading to efficient image generation is valuable to the community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of inefficient resource allocation in text-to-image generation.
    *   **Principled Approach:** The framework is based on a well-defined optimization problem with a theoretical analysis of the optimal routing rule.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments on multiple datasets (COCO and DiffusionDB) and with various quality metrics provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the approach. There's a good mix of qualitative and quantitative results.
    *   **Generalizability**: The approach is model-agnostic and could be applied to many text-to-image architectures.
    * User Study validating some of the claims.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on Pre-trained Models:** The framework relies on a set of pre-trained models.  The cost of training these initial models isn't addressed, and the performance is limited by the quality of the available models. A detailed analysis of the model selection rate for the experiment settings in Section 5 is helpful in understanding the proposed method.
    *   **Quality Estimator:** The performance of the system heavily depends on the accuracy of the quality estimator. While the paper explores two estimators (KNN and Transformer), more analysis of the limitations of these estimators in different scenarios would be beneficial. Understanding the scenarios where the quality estimator fails would highlight areas for improvement.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While the paper compares the performance of KNN against transformer, more insights could be gained from additional ablation studies.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence research in several directions:

    *   **Adaptive Computation:**  It encourages further exploration of adaptive computation techniques in generative models.
    *   **Model Routing:** It may inspire the application of model routing concepts from NLP to other areas of computer vision.
    *   **Resource-Efficient Generative Models:**  It contributes to the development of more resource-efficient generative models, making them more accessible and sustainable.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a well-motivated and executed approach to improving the efficiency of text-to-image generation. While it has some limitations, the results are promising, and the idea of cost-aware routing has the potential to be influential in the field.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper offers a non-trivial adaptation of existing adaptive computation techniques to text-to-image generation, providing a framework and empirical evidence of its effectiveness.  While the novelty is incremental, the problem being addressed is significant, and the results are strong. The reliance on pre-trained models and dependence on the quality estimator are notable weaknesses but don't negate the overall contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Scaling-Up the Pretraining of the Earth Observation Foundation Model PhilEO to the MajorTOM Dataset
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14765v1)
- **Authors**: Nikolaos Dionelis, Jente Bosmans, Riccardo Musto, Giancarlo Paoletti, Simone Sarti, Giacomo Cascarano, Casper Fibaek, Luke Camilleri, Bertrand Le Saux, Nicolas Longépé
- **Abstract**: Today, Earth Observation (EO) satellites generate massive volumes of data, with the Copernicus Sentinel-2 constellation alone producing approximately 1.6TB per day. To fully exploit this information, it is essential to pretrain EO Foundation Models (FMs) on large unlabeled datasets, enabling efficient fine-tuning for several different downstream tasks with minimal labeled data. In this work, we present the scaling-up of our recently proposed EO Foundation Model, PhilEO Geo-Aware U-Net, on the unlabeled 23TB dataset MajorTOM, which covers the vast majority of the Earth's surface, as well as on the specialized subset FastTOM 2TB that does not include oceans and ice. We develop and study various PhilEO model variants with different numbers of parameters and architectures. Finally, we fine-tune the models on the PhilEO Bench for road density estimation, building density pixel-wise regression, and land cover semantic segmentation, and we evaluate the performance. Our results demonstrate that for all n-shots for road density regression, the PhilEO 44M MajorTOM 23TB model outperforms PhilEO Globe 0.5TB 44M. We also show that for most n-shots for road density estimation and building density regression, PhilEO 200M FastTOM outperforms all the other models. The effectiveness of both dataset and model scaling is validated using the PhilEO Bench. We also study the impact of architecture scaling, transitioning from U-Net Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN) to Vision Transformers (ViT).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Scaling-Up the Pretraining of the Earth Observation Foundation Model PhilEO to the MajorTOM Dataset":

**Summary:**

This paper investigates scaling up Earth Observation Foundation Models (GFMs) using the PhilEO architecture. The authors scale both the pretraining dataset (from 0.5TB PhilEO Globe to 23TB MajorTOM and a 2TB FastTOM subset) and the model size/architecture (exploring both CNN-based U-Nets and Vision Transformer (ViT) architectures). They evaluate the impact of these scaling efforts on downstream tasks within the PhilEO Bench, which includes road density estimation, building density regression, and land cover mapping. The results demonstrate that larger pretraining datasets generally improve performance, especially in low-shot scenarios. They also compare CNN vs. ViT architectures, finding that ViT with a UPerNet decoder performs competitively with, or better than, CNN-based approaches. The paper explores the trade-offs between general-purpose vs. specialized pretraining data, showing that while specialized data can initially provide an advantage for land-focused tasks, scaling up general-purpose data can eventually lead to better performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Significant Dataset Scale:** The primary strength is the use of the 23TB MajorTOM dataset for pretraining. Utilizing such a large, unlabeled dataset is a valuable contribution, given the data-rich environment of Earth Observation.
    *   **Comprehensive Experimentation:** The paper systematically explores the impact of dataset scaling, model scaling, and architectural choices (CNN vs. ViT). This provides a thorough evaluation of the different aspects influencing GFM performance.
    *   **PhilEO Bench:** The use of the standardized PhilEO Bench allows for fair and consistent comparison across models and downstream tasks. This promotes reproducibility and facilitates future research in the area.
    *   **Architectural Exploration:** The comparison of U-Net and UPerNet decoders and the investigation of ViT-based models add to the understanding of which architectural choices are best suited for EO Foundation Models.
    *   **Ablation Study on Specialization:** The paper dives into the effect of specialization on pre-training data. This study allows for the community to better understand the tradeoff between generalizable and specialized models.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the paper presents a large-scale study, the underlying techniques (masked autoencoding, U-Net architecture, ViTs) are not particularly novel *per se*. The novelty mainly lies in the application and scaling of these techniques to EO data.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Computational Cost:** The paper mentions using HPC resources but lacks a more detailed analysis of the computational cost associated with training such large models. Providing details on training time, GPU usage, and power consumption would be valuable.
    *   **Minor Improvements in Some Cases:** While scaling improves performance, the relative gains are not always dramatic. In some cases, the simpler PhilEO Globe model still performs competitively. This raises questions about the cost-benefit ratio of extreme dataset scaling.
    *   **Limited Dataset Exploration:** The MajorTOM dataset is simply treated as a large unlabeled dataset. Further insights could be gained from performing a deeper analysis of its content and diversity.
*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper highlights the importance of large-scale unlabeled data for pretraining EO Foundation Models, which will likely be crucial for future advancements in the field.
    *   The benchmarking on the PhilEO Bench contributes to a better understanding of different GFM architectures and pretraining strategies for specific downstream tasks.
    *   The investigation of ViT architectures in this context is valuable, as ViTs are becoming increasingly popular in computer vision.
    *   The paper helps to define a better path forward for future study in pretraining data.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution to the field of EO Foundation Models. Its strengths lie in the comprehensive experimentation, the use of a large dataset, and the standardization of the PhilEO Bench. However, the novelty is somewhat limited, and the performance gains are incremental in some cases. The lack of detailed analysis of computational cost is also a drawback.

Overall, the paper is a significant engineering effort with valuable insights for the EO community. It provides a strong foundation for future research in large-scale pretraining.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Variational Framework for Improving Naturalness in Generative Spoken Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14767v1)
- **Authors**: Li-Wei Chen, Takuya Higuchi, Zakaria Aldeneh, Ahmed Hussen Abdelaziz, Alexander Rudnicky
- **Abstract**: The success of large language models in text processing has inspired their adaptation to speech modeling. However, since speech is continuous and complex, it is often discretized for autoregressive modeling. Speech tokens derived from self-supervised models (known as semantic tokens) typically focus on the linguistic aspects of speech but neglect prosodic information. As a result, models trained on these tokens can generate speech with reduced naturalness. Existing approaches try to fix this by adding pitch features to the semantic tokens. However, pitch alone cannot fully represent the range of paralinguistic attributes, and selecting the right features requires careful hand-engineering. To overcome this, we propose an end-to-end variational approach that automatically learns to encode these continuous speech attributes to enhance the semantic tokens. Our approach eliminates the need for manual extraction and selection of paralinguistic features. Moreover, it produces preferred speech continuations according to human raters. Code, samples and models are available at https://github.com/b04901014/vae-gslm.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of generating natural-sounding speech using language models.  Traditional approaches rely on discretizing speech into semantic tokens (derived from self-supervised models) which capture linguistic information but often neglect paralinguistic aspects like prosody, leading to unnatural-sounding speech.  The authors propose a variational framework that automatically learns to encode continuous speech attributes (paralinguistic features) to enhance the semantic tokens. Their approach eliminates the need for manual feature engineering and selection of paralinguistic attributes (e.g., pitch). The learned features are optimized for both speech reconstruction and autoregressive modeling of semantic tokens. Experimental results show that the proposed method generates more natural-sounding speech compared to baselines while maintaining comparable meaningfulness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using a VAE with an autoregressive prior to capture paralinguistic information *automatically* is relatively novel in the context of generative *spoken language* modeling. Previous work has focused on either manually engineering features (like pitch) or using acoustic tokens at the decoder stage, or using pre-trained models. The end-to-end approach to learning paralinguistic features within the autoregressive framework sets this work apart. However, the general concept of VAEs with autoregressive priors is not new, particularly within the image domain. The novelty lies in its application and adaptation to spoken language modeling.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its ability to improve the naturalness of generated speech without requiring extensive manual feature engineering. This makes it easier to adapt to new datasets or languages. The subjective evaluation results demonstrating improved naturalness are particularly important. The method addresses a key limitation of existing token-based speech generation approaches. While the improvement in objective language metrics (ZeroSpeech) is not substantial, the authors provide a plausible explanation: the model trades off some linguistic expressiveness to focus on capturing acoustic details, particularly those relevant to prosody.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The end-to-end approach is a major strength, reducing reliance on human expertise and simplifying the modeling pipeline.
    *   The subjective evaluations demonstrate a clear improvement in naturalness.
    *   The paper presents a well-defined architecture and provides clear ablation studies to demonstrate the contribution of different components.
    *   The discussion about the trade-off between linguistic content and paralinguistic features is insightful.
    *   Open-source code contributes to reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The gains in objective language modeling metrics (ZeroSpeech) are limited, and the authors acknowledge this limitation.
    *   While the performance is good, the model performance on ZeroSpeech metrics are worse than Token-LM. The results indicate a tradeoff between reconstruction capabilities and general language modeling as more acoustic and paralinguistic features are captured.
    *   The evaluation is limited to English speech, and the generalizability to other languages remains to be explored.
    *   The model performance is sensitive to hyperparameter settings (β and γ). Although the authors mention future work to address tuning these hyperparameters, a less sensitive method might be more practical.
    *   The reliance on discrete semantic tokens extracted from HuBERT could be a bottleneck; the VAE's ability to learn truly novel representations might be limited.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence future research in generative speech modeling by encouraging more end-to-end approaches that automatically learn to capture paralinguistic features. The idea of using a VAE to complement semantic tokens can be adopted by other researchers working on related problems.  The results serve as a reminder of the importance of naturalness in speech generation and the limitations of relying solely on linguistic information. The open-sourced code and models should also facilitate further research and development in this area.

*   **Score Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by addressing a practical challenge in speech generation and offering an elegant end-to-end solution. The improvement in subjective naturalness is significant. However, the limitations in objective language metrics and the sensitivity to hyperparameter tuning warrant a more moderate score. Also, the idea builds on existing VAE + autoregressive prior work done in computer vision, though the *application* and *adaptation* to speech generation is valuable. The limitations in cross-linguistic generalizability reduce the immediate impact, while the reliance on a pre-trained HuBERT encoder somewhat limits the model's ability to discover completely novel representations.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL

### CDP: Towards Robust Autoregressive Visuomotor Policy Learning via Causal Diffusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14769v1)
- **Authors**: Jiahua Ma, Yiran Qin, Yixiong Li, Xuanqi Liao, Yulan Guo, Ruimao Zhang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion Policy (DP) enables robots to learn complex behaviors by imitating expert demonstrations through action diffusion. However, in practical applications, hardware limitations often degrade data quality, while real-time constraints restrict model inference to instantaneous state and scene observations. These limitations seriously reduce the efficacy of learning from expert demonstrations, resulting in failures in object localization, grasp planning, and long-horizon task execution. To address these challenges, we propose Causal Diffusion Policy (CDP), a novel transformer-based diffusion model that enhances action prediction by conditioning on historical action sequences, thereby enabling more coherent and context-aware visuomotor policy learning. To further mitigate the computational cost associated with autoregressive inference, a caching mechanism is also introduced to store attention key-value pairs from previous timesteps, substantially reducing redundant computations during execution. Extensive experiments in both simulated and real-world environments, spanning diverse 2D and 3D manipulation tasks, demonstrate that CDP uniquely leverages historical action sequences to achieve significantly higher accuracy than existing methods. Moreover, even when faced with degraded input observation quality, CDP maintains remarkable precision by reasoning through temporal continuity, which highlights its practical robustness for robotic control under realistic, imperfect conditions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CDP: Towards Robust Autoregressive Visuomotor Policy Learning via Causal Diffusion":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Causal Diffusion Policy (CDP), a novel visuomotor policy learning framework built upon diffusion models. CDP aims to improve the robustness and coherence of robot action generation by conditioning action prediction on historical action sequences. This addresses limitations of existing diffusion policy methods that treat actions independently, leading to error accumulation and instability. The framework uses a transformer-based diffusion model with a causal temporal attention mechanism to capture temporal dependencies. Additionally, it employs a caching mechanism to improve inference efficiency by reusing attention key-value pairs from previous timesteps, reducing redundant computations. Extensive experiments in simulated and real-world environments demonstrate that CDP outperforms existing methods, especially in challenging conditions with degraded observation quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonable amount of novelty.

    *   Introducing historical action context in diffusion policies for robotics is a good step forward and potentially helpful. Adding an autoregressive element and causal reasoning in a diffusion-based robotic control framework appears to be a useful extension.

    *   The caching mechanism to improve inference efficiency is also a practical contribution, although it is not necessarily a groundbreaking idea. Similar caching mechanisms have been used in other sequence models, but applying it effectively in this context has value.

*   **Significance and Impact:**

    *   The paper's significance stems from addressing practical limitations of diffusion-based robot learning in real-world scenarios. The improvements in robustness and coherence achieved by CDP could have a tangible impact on the deployment of robots in environments with noisy sensors and imperfect data.

    *   The experimental results seem promising, demonstrating clear advantages over baseline methods in various manipulation tasks, with particularly good demonstration in real-world experiments. This validates the core idea of the CDP architecture, suggesting the value of leveraging historical action context.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The core idea of incorporating historical context is well-motivated and addresses a clear limitation of existing diffusion policies.
    *   The design of the causal temporal attention mechanism seems effective in capturing temporal dependencies.
    *   The experimental results in both simulation and real-world environments provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of CDP.
    *   The caching mechanism enhances the practical feasibility of CDP by reducing inference time.
    *   The paper provides clear explanations of the method and the experimental setup.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The method relies heavily on the availability of expert demonstrations.
    *   The ablation study on the length of historical actions and target actions is somewhat limited. More detailed analysis is required to understand the optimal configuration for different tasks.
    *   While the paper mentions limitations regarding long-horizon tasks, there is a need for further discussion on the potential limitations and failure modes of the CDP approach.

*   **Rigorous Evaluation Justification:**
    The paper demonstrates sufficient novelty and provides significant improvements over existing methods for robotic manipulation tasks. While some components (like caching) are relatively common in sequence modeling, the specific architecture for the causal diffusion policy (CDP) makes improvements, and the integration of historical context is presented well. Furthermore, experiments present the usefulness of the integration and improves the performance of real robots. The paper is a positive contribution to the field, but there is room for significant improvement and expansion on future works.

Score: 7.0

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### DETONATE: A Benchmark for Text-to-Image Alignment and Kernelized Direct Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14903v1)
- **Authors**: Renjith Prasad, Abhilekh Borah, Hasnat Md Abdullah, Chathurangi Shyalika, Gurpreet Singh, Ritvik Garimella, Rajarshi Roy, Harshul Surana, Nasrin Imanpour, Suranjana Trivedy, Amit Sheth, Amitava Das
- **Abstract**: Alignment is crucial for text-to-image (T2I) models to ensure that generated images faithfully capture user intent while maintaining safety and fairness. Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), prominent in large language models (LLMs), is extending its influence to T2I systems. This paper introduces DPO-Kernels for T2I models, a novel extension enhancing alignment across three dimensions: (i) Hybrid Loss, integrating embedding-based objectives with traditional probability-based loss for improved optimization; (ii) Kernelized Representations, employing Radial Basis Function (RBF), Polynomial, and Wavelet kernels for richer feature transformations and better separation between safe and unsafe inputs; and (iii) Divergence Selection, expanding beyond DPO's default Kullback-Leibler (KL) regularizer by incorporating Wasserstein and R'enyi divergences for enhanced stability and robustness. We introduce DETONATE, the first large-scale benchmark of its kind, comprising approximately 100K curated image pairs categorized as chosen and rejected. DETONATE encapsulates three axes of social bias and discrimination: Race, Gender, and Disability. Prompts are sourced from hate speech datasets, with images generated by leading T2I models including Stable Diffusion 3.5 Large, Stable Diffusion XL, and Midjourney. Additionally, we propose the Alignment Quality Index (AQI), a novel geometric measure quantifying latent-space separability of safe/unsafe image activations, revealing hidden vulnerabilities. Empirically, we demonstrate that DPO-Kernels maintain strong generalization bounds via Heavy-Tailed Self-Regularization (HT-SR). DETONATE and complete code are publicly released.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DPO-Kernels, a novel extension of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) designed to improve alignment in text-to-image (T2I) models.  It addresses limitations in current alignment techniques by focusing on structural regularization in representation space rather than post-hoc filtering or scalar preferences. DPO-Kernels incorporates three key components: (1) a hybrid loss combining embedding-based objectives with probability-based loss, (2) kernelized representations using Radial Basis Function (RBF), Polynomial, and Wavelet kernels, and (3) divergence selection, expanding beyond the Kullback-Leibler (KL) regularizer to include alternatives like Wasserstein and Rényi divergences. The authors also present DETONATE, a large-scale benchmark comprising 100K image pairs categorized as chosen/rejected based on social bias (Race, Gender, Disability), and the Alignment Quality Index (AQI), a geometric measure for evaluating latent space separability. Their experiments demonstrate that DPO-Kernels achieves better alignment compared to existing methods and strong generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several areas. First, the explicit integration of kernel methods into DPO for T2I alignment is a significant departure from standard DPO approaches that operate on scalar preferences. Kernel methods enable a richer understanding of data structure by creating embedding losses. Second, the introduction of the AQI as an evaluation metric that probes the latent space for geometric separability is a valuable contribution, going beyond surface-level metrics. Third, the DETONATE benchmark is a significant addition to the field, providing a resource for studying bias and fairness in T2I models, in axes that require sensitive attention (gender, race, disability) that have not been widely discussed in the literature.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical issue in T2I generation: ensuring alignment with user intent while maintaining safety and fairness. The rise of AI-generated content means that misalignment can have significant real-world consequences. The use of DPO is a popular and effective approach for text-conditioned models, so kernelizing it offers a promising step forward.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The paper grounds its approach in reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHS) theory and heavy-tailed self-regularization, which strengthen the justification for their design choices.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results on two models and diverse metrics, demonstrating the effectiveness of DPO-Kernels. The analysis of different kernel choices and divergence measures provides valuable insights.
    *   **Practical Contributions:** The DETONATE benchmark and AQI metric are valuable resources that the community can leverage for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper acknowledges the increased computational overhead of DPO-Kernels, but a more thorough analysis of the scalability limitations and potential mitigation strategies would strengthen the work. Although it's mentioned there are approximation techniques in the appendix, this could be elaborated on in the main paper.
    *   **Kernel Collapse:** Kernel collapse is discussed as a possibility, but not fully addressed in experimentation. More analysis of conditions that promote collapse and methods to address it is warranted.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Hyperparameter Tuning**: The discussion of hyperparameters is brief, and it's unclear how sensitive the results are to specific parameter choices.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   **Shift in Perspective:** The emphasis on structural alignment in latent space can help to move the field beyond symptomatic alignment fixes.
    *   **New Evaluation Approaches:** The introduction of AQI and DETONATE benchmark are likely to impact how T2I alignment is evaluated in the future.
    *   **Practical Applications:**  The DPO-Kernels framework has the potential to be adopted in real-world T2I systems to improve safety and fairness.

The paper is well-written and provides clear explanations of the proposed methods. Overall, this is a strong paper that makes significant contributions to the field of T2I alignment.

Score: 8

**Rationale:**
The paper's strengths lie in its solid theoretical foundations, valuable benchmark and evaluation metric, and comprehensive experimental evaluation. The application of kernel methods to DPO for T2I is a technically sound and novel approach. The identified weaknesses are primarily related to limitations around computational cost and further parameter analysis; these are important, but do not overshadow the core contribution. The paper has the potential to shift the focus in T2I alignment research towards structural regularization. This makes it more than just a mere incremental improvement, but a high potential contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### CrEst: Credibility Estimation for Contexts in LLMs via Weak Supervision
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14912v1)
- **Authors**: Dyah Adila, Shuai Zhang, Boran Han, Bonan Min, Yuyang Wang
- **Abstract**: The integration of contextual information has significantly enhanced the performance of large language models (LLMs) on knowledge-intensive tasks. However, existing methods often overlook a critical challenge: the credibility of context documents can vary widely, potentially leading to the propagation of unreliable information. In this paper, we introduce CrEst, a novel weakly supervised framework for assessing the credibility of context documents during LLM inference--without requiring manual annotations. Our approach is grounded in the insight that credible documents tend to exhibit higher semantic coherence with other credible documents, enabling automated credibility estimation through inter-document agreement. To incorporate credibility into LLM inference, we propose two integration strategies: a black-box approach for models without access to internal weights or activations, and a white-box method that directly modifies attention mechanisms. Extensive experiments across three model architectures and five datasets demonstrate that CrEst consistently outperforms strong baselines, achieving up to a 26.86% improvement in accuracy and a 3.49% increase in F1 score. Further analysis shows that CrEst maintains robust performance even under high-noise conditions.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CrEst, a novel weakly supervised framework for estimating the credibility of context documents used in large language model (LLM) inference. CrEst leverages the intuition that credible documents tend to exhibit higher semantic coherence with other credible documents within the in-context document set. This inter-document agreement is used to automatically estimate document credibility without manual annotations. The paper proposes two integration strategies for incorporating these credibility estimates into LLM inference: a black-box approach suitable for models with restricted access and a white-box approach for models allowing direct manipulation of attention mechanisms. The authors demonstrate through experiments across various datasets and model architectures that CrEst consistently outperforms strong baselines, achieving significant improvements in accuracy and F1 score, even under noisy conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using weak supervision to estimate the credibility of context documents is reasonably novel.  While weak supervision itself is a well-established technique, its application to context credibility assessment in LLMs, particularly *without* relying on model-generated signals or human annotations is a clear contribution. The two integration strategies (black-box and white-box) are also reasonably novel, tailoring the use of credibility scores to different levels of model access.  The novelty hinges on the specific application and combination of these elements.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in addressing a critical challenge in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG): the trustworthiness of retrieved context. The paper offers a practical solution that improves the robustness of LLMs against noisy or misleading context documents, which can significantly impact performance.  The empirical results support the claim that CrEst enhances accuracy and F1 score across a variety of benchmarks. This signifies a valuable step towards making RAG systems more reliable and trustworthy. However, it's crucial to consider the limitations of the proposed approach.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Weakly Supervised Approach:** The approach avoids the need for manual annotations, making it more scalable and applicable to large datasets.
    *   **Computational Efficiency:** Compared to methods requiring separate model queries for each document, CrEst's parallel processing of documents is computationally efficient.
    *   **Black-Box and White-Box Integration:** The provision of two integration strategies caters to different model architectures and access levels, increasing the practical applicability of CrEst.
    *   **Robustness to Noise:** The method demonstrates robustness to noise in context documents.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The performance improvements are clearly demonstrated across various datasets and models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Semantic Coherence:** The assumption that credible documents exhibit higher semantic coherence might not hold in all cases. As acknowledged in the paper, if the *majority* of retrieved documents are unreliable, the method might incorrectly assign high credibility scores to these documents. While the paper claims that this is rare, more extensive investigation under diverse scenarios is required.
    *   **Dependence on Embedding Models:** The performance of CrEst is heavily dependent on the quality and choice of embedding models. While ensembling is used to improve robustness, the gains plateau after a few embedders. This may necessitate careful selection and tuning of the embedding models for different tasks and domains.
    *   **Limited Scope of Limitations Discussion:** The paper acknowledges limitations, but a deeper dive into scenarios where CrEst may fail and a more comprehensive analysis of the error modes would further strengthen the paper.
    *   **Lack of theoretical justification:** While the empirical results are impressive, a more robust theoretical foundation for the proposed method would be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** CrEst has the potential to influence the direction of research in retrieval-augmented generation and context-aware LLMs. It provides a valuable framework for addressing the problem of noisy context information and paves the way for further exploration of weakly supervised techniques in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of RAG, several weaknesses prevent it from achieving a very high score. The reliance on semantic coherence and the potential for inflated credibility scores under specific conditions are important limitations. Additionally, more extensive ablation studies and error analyses would further strengthen the claims. Overall, the paper demonstrates a valuable practical approach with promising results, but with areas for further development and improvement.

### Winter Soldier: Backdooring Language Models at Pre-Training with Indirect Data Poisoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14913v1)
- **Authors**: Wassim Bouaziz, Mathurin Videau, Nicolas Usunier, El-Mahdi El-Mhamdi
- **Abstract**: The pre-training of large language models (LLMs) relies on massive text datasets sourced from diverse and difficult-to-curate origins. Although membership inference attacks and hidden canaries have been explored to trace data usage, such methods rely on memorization of training data, which LM providers try to limit. In this work, we demonstrate that indirect data poisoning (where the targeted behavior is absent from training data) is not only feasible but also allow to effectively protect a dataset and trace its use. Using gradient-based optimization prompt-tuning, we make a model learn arbitrary secret sequences: secret responses to secret prompts that are absent from the training corpus. We validate our approach on language models pre-trained from scratch and show that less than 0.005% of poisoned tokens are sufficient to covertly make a LM learn a secret and detect it with extremely high confidence ($p < 10^{-55}$) with a theoretically certifiable scheme. Crucially, this occurs without performance degradation (on LM benchmarks) and despite secrets never appearing in the training set.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to backdooring language models (LLMs) during pre-training using indirect data poisoning. Unlike traditional methods that rely on memorization or explicit triggers in the training data, this technique subtly alters the training data to induce the model to associate specific "secret" prompts with targeted responses *without ever including those prompt-response pairs in the training set*. This allows the model owner (Bob) to be unaware that this effect has occurred and enables verification of the training by the dataset owner (Alice) after training. The method leverages gradient-based optimization and prompt tuning to craft poisonous samples that guide the model to learn the desired associations. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach on language models trained from scratch, showing that a small percentage of poisoned tokens can covertly make a language model learn a secret sequence while maintaining performance on standard benchmarks. They also provide a theoretically certifiable scheme for detecting such backdoors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The key novelty of this work lies in its use of *indirect* data poisoning. It moves beyond direct injection of backdoors or canaries, which are susceptible to detection and removal. The approach of using gradient-based prompt tuning to subtly modify the training data to enforce secret prompt-response mappings absent from the training set itself is quite innovative. The theoretical certification that the model can be verified without the knowledge of the operator of the model after training is novel as well. It builds on the idea of data taggants from the image domain [4], and adapts it successfully to the text modality, addressing the challenge of discrete token spaces through Gumbel-Softmax reparameterization. This has implications for dataset ownership and data provenance verification.

**Significance:** The paper's significance stems from several factors:

*   **Dataset Ownership Verification:** It offers a practical way for dataset owners to verify if their data has been used to train a model, even when the model is a black box. This is crucial in the age of large language models trained on diverse and often poorly curated data.
*   **Stealth:** The indirect nature of the poisoning makes the backdoor difficult to detect using standard techniques like filtering memorized sequences.  This makes it potentially a very powerful attack.
*   **Theoretical Guarantee:**  The provided theoretical framework enables computation of a certifiable false detection rate, providing confidence in the detection mechanism.
*   **Practicality:** The experiments demonstrate the feasibility of the attack with a relatively small number of poisoned tokens and without significant performance degradation.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Threat Model Assumptions:** The threat model relies on Alice's knowledge of Bob's model architecture and tokenizer, which can be a limiting factor. The authors acknowledge the challenge in cases where there is a mismatch of the tokenizer of Alice's dataset and Bob's model. A truly universal approach needs to address this.
*   **Computational Cost:** Crafting the poisonous samples is computationally intensive, potentially limiting the applicability of the approach.
*   **Limited Stealthiness:** As the authors also point out, the current implementation of the method provides limited stealthiness of the crafted poisons which can be detected by using a perplexity filter or a quality classifier. However, these filters do not completely eliminate the threat of a successful attack, but it might be possible that it will make an attack more difficult for Alice.
*   **Focus on pre-training:** The primary demonstration is within pre-training. The impact on subsequent fine-tuning scenarios is not thoroughly explored. A potential avenue is the use of this technology during fine-tuning for an easier stealthiness and implementation of this backdoor.

**Score Justification:**

Overall, the paper presents a compelling and novel approach to backdooring language models. The concept of indirect data poisoning, the experimental validation, and the theoretical certification are valuable contributions. The weaknesses related to threat model assumptions and computational cost slightly diminish the overall impact. Given this assessment, the paper merits a score of **8.** This reflects a significant contribution to the field with the caveats that stealthiness needs to be improved and the threat model needs to be generalized.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Frequency-Calibrated Membership Inference Attacks on Medical Image Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14919v1)
- **Authors**: Xinkai Zhao, Yuta Tokuoka, Junichiro Iwasawa, Keita Oda
- **Abstract**: The increasing use of diffusion models for image generation, especially in sensitive areas like medical imaging, has raised significant privacy concerns. Membership Inference Attack (MIA) has emerged as a potential approach to determine if a specific image was used to train a diffusion model, thus quantifying privacy risks. Existing MIA methods often rely on diffusion reconstruction errors, where member images are expected to have lower reconstruction errors than non-member images. However, applying these methods directly to medical images faces challenges. Reconstruction error is influenced by inherent image difficulty, and diffusion models struggle with high-frequency detail reconstruction. To address these issues, we propose a Frequency-Calibrated Reconstruction Error (FCRE) method for MIAs on medical image diffusion models. By focusing on reconstruction errors within a specific mid-frequency range and excluding both high-frequency (difficult to reconstruct) and low-frequency (less informative) regions, our frequency-selective approach mitigates the confounding factor of inherent image difficulty. Specifically, we analyze the reverse diffusion process, obtain the mid-frequency reconstruction error, and compute the structural similarity index score between the reconstructed and original images. Membership is determined by comparing this score to a threshold. Experiments on several medical image datasets demonstrate that our FCRE method outperforms existing MIA methods.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of membership inference attacks (MIAs) against diffusion models used for generating medical images.  Existing MIA methods often rely on reconstruction error to determine if an image was used in training, but these methods struggle with medical images due to their inherent difficulty and the limitations of diffusion models in reconstructing high-frequency details. The authors propose a Frequency-Calibrated Reconstruction Error (FCRE) method that focuses on reconstruction errors within a specific mid-frequency range, excluding both high-frequency (difficult to reconstruct) and low-frequency (less informative) regions.  They analyze the reverse diffusion process, obtain the mid-frequency reconstruction error, and use the Structural Similarity Index (SSIM) score between reconstructed and original images for membership inference.  Experiments on medical image datasets (FeTS 2022, Chest X-ray8) show that FCRE outperforms existing MIA methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in adapting the concept of "difficulty calibration," previously used in other MIA contexts, to the specific challenges of medical image diffusion models. The key innovation is the frequency-selective reconstruction error analysis, leveraging the spectral characteristics of medical images to improve MIA performance. While the individual components (SSIM, diffusion model inversion) are not new, their integration within the proposed FCRE framework and specifically tailored to medical image MIA is novel. This is a **meaningful adaptation** rather than a completely new theoretical framework.

**Significance:**

The significance of this work lies in several areas:

*   **Addressing a Practical Vulnerability:** MIAs pose a real privacy threat to diffusion models trained on sensitive medical data. This paper provides a more effective means of assessing this vulnerability, which is crucial for developing robust and privacy-preserving models.
*   **Tailored to Medical Imaging:** The proposed method directly addresses the specific characteristics of medical images, which often differ significantly from natural images. This targeted approach is valuable and likely to lead to more accurate risk assessments.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments provide convincing evidence that FCRE outperforms existing MIA methods on relevant medical image datasets. This empirical validation strengthens the paper's claims and demonstrates the practical benefits of the proposed approach.
*   **Highlighting a Need:** The paper further motivates research into defense mechanisms for medical image diffusion models, contributing to responsible development within this field.

**Strengths:**

*   Clearly defined problem and motivation.
*   A well-explained and conceptually sound approach.
*   Effective use of existing techniques in a novel way.
*   Convincing experimental results on relevant datasets.
*   Clear writing and presentation.
*   Ablation studies further strengthen the validity of the work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   While the mid-frequency selection seems well-reasoned, the method relies on hyperparameters (Lmin, Lmax) that may require tuning for different datasets. The paper could have benefited from exploring adaptive thresholding methods or providing more general guidelines for selecting these hyperparameters.
*   The method builds upon existing techniques like DDIM and SSIM.  While their integration is novel in this context, the fundamental building blocks are not groundbreaking.
*   While tested on MRI and Chest X-Rays, experiments could extend to other medical image modalities such as CT scans or Ultrasound.

**Potential Influence:**

This paper has the potential to influence future research in the following ways:

*   It establishes a new benchmark for MIAs on medical image diffusion models.
*   It inspires further exploration of frequency-based techniques for privacy analysis in medical imaging.
*   It motivates the development of defense mechanisms against MIAs in medical image generation.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid and practical contribution to the field of privacy analysis in medical imaging. Its novelty lies in the thoughtful adaptation of existing techniques to address the specific challenges of medical image diffusion models, demonstrating its value through well-designed experiments. While the core components are not fundamentally novel, the problem is relevant, the solution is effective, and the experimental results are convincing. The primary limitation is its reliance on a manually tuned frequency range. Overall, it's a significant step forward in understanding and mitigating privacy risks in this domain.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### FORTRESS: Frontier Risk Evaluation for National Security and Public Safety
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14922v1)
- **Authors**: Christina Q. Knight, Kaustubh Deshpande, Ved Sirdeshmukh, Meher Mankikar, Scale Red Team, SEAL Research Team, Julian Michael
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) introduces dual-use capabilities that could both threaten and bolster national security and public safety (NSPS). Models implement safeguards to protect against potential misuse relevant to NSPS and allow for benign users to receive helpful information. However, current benchmarks often fail to test safeguard robustness to potential NSPS risks in an objective, robust way. We introduce FORTRESS: 500 expert-crafted adversarial prompts with instance-based rubrics of 4-7 binary questions for automated evaluation across 3 domains (unclassified information only): Chemical, Biological, Radiological, Nuclear and Explosive (CBRNE), Political Violence & Terrorism, and Criminal & Financial Illicit Activities, with 10 total subcategories across these domains. Each prompt-rubric pair has a corresponding benign version to test for model over-refusals. This evaluation of frontier LLMs' safeguard robustness reveals varying trade-offs between potential risks and model usefulness: Claude-3.5-Sonnet demonstrates a low average risk score (ARS) (14.09 out of 100) but the highest over-refusal score (ORS) (21.8 out of 100), while Gemini 2.5 Pro shows low over-refusal (1.4) but a high average potential risk (66.29). Deepseek-R1 has the highest ARS at 78.05, but the lowest ORS at only 0.06. Models such as o1 display a more even trade-off between potential risks and over-refusals (with an ARS of 21.69 and ORS of 5.2). To provide policymakers and researchers with a clear understanding of models' potential risks, we publicly release FORTRESS at https://huggingface.co/datasets/ScaleAI/fortress_public. We also maintain a private set for evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FORTRESS, a novel benchmark for evaluating the robustness of large language model (LLM) safeguards against misuse relevant to national security and public safety (NSPS). The benchmark consists of 500 expert-crafted adversarial prompts across three domains: Chemical, Biological, Radiological, Nuclear, and Explosive (CBRNE); Political Violence & Terrorism; and Criminal & Financial Illicit Activities.  Each prompt is paired with a benign counterpart to test for over-refusals and instance-specific rubrics with 4-7 binary questions for automated evaluation.  The authors evaluate the safeguards of several state-of-the-art LLMs, revealing trade-offs between potential risks and model usefulness. The FORTRESS dataset (a public and a private set) is made publicly available to promote further research and provide policymakers with a clear understanding of models' potential risks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates moderate novelty. While benchmarks for LLM safety are not new, FORTRESS focuses specifically on NSPS risks, an area that has been relatively under-represented in existing benchmarks. The instance-specific rubrics are a good idea to automate the evaluation while keeping the answers close to domain expertise.  Including a paired benign prompt is also a good practice to evaluate over-refusal. That said, one could argue, that many prompts may boil down to simple token-based filtering which is easy to identify, even without domain expertise.

*   **Significance:** The significance is considerable. NSPS is an extremely important area, and the development of robust and reliable benchmarks is crucial for responsible AI development. FORTRESS offers a valuable tool for evaluating LLMs in this domain, potentially informing policy decisions and guiding developers in improving safeguard mechanisms. By highlighting the trade-offs between safety and utility, the benchmark encourages a more nuanced approach to LLM safety. The release of the dataset has value because it provides a shared and comparable way to compare state-of-the-art models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The focus on NSPS risks fills an important gap in existing benchmarks.
    *   The use of expert-crafted adversarial prompts and instance-based rubrics enhances the realism and accuracy of the evaluation.
    *   The inclusion of benign prompts addresses the critical issue of over-refusal, promoting a balance between safety and utility.
    *   The public release of the dataset facilitates further research and collaboration.
    *   Rigorous evaluation using well-defined metrics.
    *   The evaluation takes into account the interplay between the model performance and the adversarial tactic utilized.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The benchmark is limited to static, single-turn prompts, which may not fully capture the complexity of real-world interactions and misuse scenarios. More complex interactions (multi-turn jailbreaks, using agents) would provide a more thorough and robust testing environment.
    *   The expert annotators lack the same knowledge as actors with mal-intent which may affect nuances in prompts and interpretation of outputs.
    *   The data set contains only 500 adversarial and 500 benign prompts, which may not sufficiently cover all potential NSPS risks or attack vectors. While 500 examples for each class is a good start, it is still limited. A larger, more diverse dataset would improve the statistical robustness of the findings. The paper mentions having a larger private set but does not share any statistics on that.

*   **Potential Influence:** The FORTRESS benchmark has the potential to influence LLM development, policy, and research in the following ways:
    *   Guiding developers in improving safeguard mechanisms for NSPS-related risks.
    *   Informing policy decisions related to the responsible deployment of LLMs.
    *   Encouraging further research on LLM safety and security.
    *   Providing a shared framework for evaluating and comparing LLMs in the NSPS domain.

**Justification for Score:**

FORTRESS presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM safety by focusing on the critical area of national security and public safety. While limited by its single-turn nature and relatively small dataset size, the benchmark offers a nuanced approach to evaluation by considering both adversarial robustness and over-refusal, and uses instance-specific rubrics to evaluate responses more carefully. Its potential to influence LLM development, policy, and research warrants a score that reflects its significance and novelty, but one that is also tempered by its limitations. It is a solid contribution to a growing area of research.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### Vision Transformers for End-to-End Quark-Gluon Jet Classification from Calorimeter Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14934v1)
- **Authors**: Md Abrar Jahin, Shahriar Soudeep, Arian Rahman Aditta, M. F. Mridha, Nafiz Fahad, Md. Jakir Hossen
- **Abstract**: Distinguishing between quark- and gluon-initiated jets is a critical and challenging task in high-energy physics, pivotal for improving new physics searches and precision measurements at the Large Hadron Collider. While deep learning, particularly Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), has advanced jet tagging using image-based representations, the potential of Vision Transformer (ViT) architectures, renowned for modeling global contextual information, remains largely underexplored for direct calorimeter image analysis, especially under realistic detector and pileup conditions. This paper presents a systematic evaluation of ViTs and ViT-CNN hybrid models for quark-gluon jet classification using simulated 2012 CMS Open Data. We construct multi-channel jet-view images from detector-level energy deposits (ECAL, HCAL) and reconstructed tracks, enabling an end-to-end learning approach. Our comprehensive benchmarking demonstrates that ViT-based models, notably ViT+MaxViT and ViT+ConvNeXt hybrids, consistently outperform established CNN baselines in F1-score, ROC-AUC, and accuracy, highlighting the advantage of capturing long-range spatial correlations within jet substructure. This work establishes the first systematic framework and robust performance baselines for applying ViT architectures to calorimeter image-based jet classification using public collider data, alongside a structured dataset suitable for further deep learning research in this domain.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of Vision Transformers (ViTs) and ViT-CNN hybrid models for quark-gluon jet classification using calorimeter images derived from simulated 2012 CMS Open Data.  It compares the performance of these architectures against CNN baselines, focusing on their ability to capture long-range spatial correlations in jet substructure.  The authors construct multi-channel "jet-view" images from ECAL, HCAL, and track data. Their experiments demonstrate that ViT-based models generally outperform CNNs in F1-score, ROC-AUC, and accuracy, suggesting the benefits of global context awareness. The paper provides a systematic framework and establishes performance baselines for applying ViTs to calorimeter image-based jet classification, alongside a publicly available dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic application and benchmarking of ViTs and ViT-CNN hybrids to calorimeter image-based jet classification using realistic CMS Open Data. While ViTs have been explored in HEP for particle cloud data, their direct application to calorimeter images, particularly with public collider data, is less explored. Constructing and making available the structured image dataset from CMS Open Data is also a valuable contribution. However, ViTs are already well-established in computer vision. The innovation resides in their adaptation to this specific HEP problem and data type, not in fundamental algorithmic advances.
*   **Significance:** Distinguishing between quark- and gluon-initiated jets is a fundamental task in high-energy physics with implications for new physics searches and precision measurements. Showing that ViTs provide an edge in this area can significantly impact how jet tagging is approached. The open-source code and dataset lower the barrier to entry for researchers interested in applying deep learning to HEP. The paper's performance comparisons and ablation studies provide valuable insights for future model development and hyperparameter tuning. The demonstration that ViTs can effectively capture global context in calorimeter images opens the door for further explorations of attention-based models in this domain.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive benchmarking of various architectures, including CNNs, ViTs, and hybrids.
    *   Systematic approach with ablation studies exploring hyperparameter sensitivity.
    *   Use of realistic, publicly available CMS Open Data.
    *   Open-source code and a structured dataset for reproducibility and future research.
    *   Clear presentation and well-structured experiments.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The study relies on simulated data. Validation with real experimental data would strengthen the findings.
    *   The ViT architectures used are not particularly novel in themselves. The paper leverages existing models rather than proposing new transformer variants specifically tailored for this task.
    *   Limited physical interpretation of the learned features. While the paper shows *that* ViTs perform well, it doesn't deeply investigate *why*. Understanding what the ViTs are learning from the images could provide insights into jet substructure and detector physics.
*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence how jet tagging is done in HEP, specifically encouraging researchers to explore and adopt attention-based models for calorimeter image analysis. The availability of the dataset and code should facilitate further research in this area.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper represents a solid contribution to the field by systematically demonstrating the effectiveness of ViTs for a crucial HEP task using realistic data. The creation and publication of the dataset and code enhance reproducibility and facilitate future research. While the algorithmic novelty is somewhat limited (relying on pre-existing ViT architectures), the paper's comprehensive benchmarking, ablation studies, and open-source nature make it a valuable resource. However, the reliance on simulated data and the limited physical interpretation of results prevent it from achieving a higher score. Testing on real experimental data and in-depth investigation into the models' learnt features is necessary to significantly improve this work.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Structured Moral Reasoning in Language Models: A Value-Grounded Evaluation Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14948v1)
- **Authors**: Mohna Chakraborty, Lu Wang, David Jurgens
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in domains requiring moral understanding, yet their reasoning often remains shallow, and misaligned with human reasoning. Unlike humans, whose moral reasoning integrates contextual trade-offs, value systems, and ethical theories, LLMs often rely on surface patterns, leading to biased decisions in morally and ethically complex scenarios. To address this gap, we present a value-grounded framework for evaluating and distilling structured moral reasoning in LLMs. We benchmark 12 open-source models across four moral datasets using a taxonomy of prompts grounded in value systems, ethical theories, and cognitive reasoning strategies. Our evaluation is guided by four questions: (1) Does reasoning improve LLM decision-making over direct prompting? (2) Which types of value/ethical frameworks most effectively guide LLM reasoning? (3) Which cognitive reasoning strategies lead to better moral performance? (4) Can small-sized LLMs acquire moral competence through distillation? We find that prompting with explicit moral structure consistently improves accuracy and coherence, with first-principles reasoning and Schwartz's + care-ethics scaffolds yielding the strongest gains. Furthermore, our supervised distillation approach transfers moral competence from large to small models without additional inference cost. Together, our results offer a scalable path toward interpretable and value-grounded models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a value-grounded framework for evaluating and improving moral reasoning in large language models (LLMs). It introduces a taxonomy of prompts grounded in value systems, ethical theories, and cognitive reasoning strategies and benchmarks 12 open-source LLMs across four moral datasets.  The study investigates whether structured moral prompting improves LLM decision-making compared to direct prompting, identifies the most effective value/ethical frameworks and cognitive reasoning strategies, and explores the possibility of transferring moral competence from large to small LLMs using knowledge distillation.  The authors find that prompting with explicit moral structure consistently improves accuracy and coherence, with first-principles reasoning and Schwartz's value system combined with care ethics showing the strongest gains. They also demonstrate that supervised distillation can transfer moral reasoning capabilities from large to small models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic approach to evaluating and improving moral reasoning in LLMs by explicitly grounding prompts in established value systems and ethical theories.  While prior work has explored moral reasoning in LLMs and prompting techniques such as chain-of-thought, this paper provides a more structured and theoretically informed approach to eliciting ethical considerations. The development and application of the taxonomy of moral reasoning prompts, particularly the combination of value systems and ethical theories, represents a valuable contribution. The distillation of moral reasoning capabilities is another positive, showing that a form of general moral reasoning ability is achievable. The detailed comparative analysis across multiple models and datasets adds to the paper's empirical strength.

* **Significance:**  The increasing deployment of LLMs in ethically sensitive domains underscores the significance of this research. By providing a framework for evaluating and improving moral reasoning, the paper addresses a crucial need for more responsible and ethically aligned AI systems. The findings suggest that LLMs can be made more context-sensitive and norm-aware by incorporating explicit moral framing, and that moral competence can be transferred to smaller models. This has implications for building more interpretable, value-grounded, and scalable AI systems. The structured experimental design offers clear guidance to practitioners seeking to enhance LLMs' ethical performance. The findings regarding which prompting techniques and frameworks were most effective offers practical guidance.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper employs a systematic and well-defined methodology, including a comprehensive taxonomy of prompts, a diverse set of LLMs and datasets, and a clearly defined evaluation framework.
    * **Theoretical Grounding:** The study is grounded in established theories of moral psychology and ethics, providing a solid foundation for the analysis and interpretation of results.
    * **Practical Implications:** The findings offer practical guidance for improving moral reasoning in LLMs, with specific recommendations for prompt design and knowledge distillation.
    * **Scalability:** The findings promote more manageable LLMs, with manageable resources and costs.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Ethical Frameworks:** While the paper incorporates a range of ethical frameworks, the selection is not exhaustive, and alternative perspectives (especially those from non-Western traditions) may offer further insights. The paper should acknowledge this limitation more explicitly.
    * **Dataset Limitations:** The reliance on existing moral datasets, while necessary, may not fully capture the complexities and nuances of real-world moral scenarios.  The potential biases embedded within these datasets are also a concern.
    * **Generalizability of Distillation:** The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of distillation in transferring moral competence to smaller models, but further research is needed to assess the generalizability of these findings across different model architectures and task domains.
     * **Single-Turn Settings:** Future work should extend evaluations to more complex, interactive, multi-turn settings to capture more realistic AI scenarios.

* **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field by providing a valuable framework for evaluating and improving moral reasoning in LLMs. The findings will be of interest to researchers and practitioners working on responsible AI, ethical AI, and natural language processing.  The paper's emphasis on structured prompting and knowledge distillation will likely inspire further research in these areas.

* **Justification of Score:**  The paper makes a solid and original contribution to the rapidly growing field of ethical AI and LLMs. Its methodological rigor, clear presentation of results, and insightful discussion support the application. While there are limitations, they are well acknowledged, and the work pushes the field significantly.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Chat to Checkup: Can Large Language Models Assist in Diabetes Prediction?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14949v1)
- **Authors**: Shadman Sakib, Oishy Fatema Akhand, Ajwad Abrar
- **Abstract**: While Machine Learning (ML) and Deep Learning (DL) models have been widely used for diabetes prediction, the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for structured numerical data is still not well explored. In this study, we test the effectiveness of LLMs in predicting diabetes using zero-shot, one-shot, and three-shot prompting methods. We conduct an empirical analysis using the Pima Indian Diabetes Database (PIDD). We evaluate six LLMs, including four open-source models: Gemma-2-27B, Mistral-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, and Llama-3.2-2B. We also test two proprietary models: GPT-4o and Gemini Flash 2.0. In addition, we compare their performance with three traditional machine learning models: Random Forest, Logistic Regression, and Support Vector Machine (SVM). We use accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score as evaluation metrics. Our results show that proprietary LLMs perform better than open-source ones, with GPT-4o and Gemma-2-27B achieving the highest accuracy in few-shot settings. Notably, Gemma-2-27B also outperforms the traditional ML models in terms of F1-score. However, there are still issues such as performance variation across prompting strategies and the need for domain-specific fine-tuning. This study shows that LLMs can be useful for medical prediction tasks and encourages future work on prompt engineering and hybrid approaches to improve healthcare predictions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) for diabetes prediction using the Pima Indian Diabetes Database (PIDD).  It evaluates six LLMs (Gemma-2-27B, Mistral-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, Llama-3.2-2B, GPT-40, and Gemini Flash 2.0) under zero-shot, one-shot, and three-shot prompting scenarios. The study compares LLM performance against traditional machine learning models (Logistic Regression, Random Forest, SVM) using accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score. The results indicate that proprietary LLMs (GPT-40, Gemma-2-27B) perform better than open-source models in few-shot settings, with Gemma-2-27B even outperforming traditional ML models in F1-score. The authors conclude that LLMs show promise for medical prediction tasks, but challenges remain regarding performance variation and the need for domain-specific tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has limited novelty.  While using LLMs for structured data is an active area of research, applying them to the well-studied PIDD dataset is not a groundbreaking contribution. Several papers already explore ML/DL models for diabetes prediction on this dataset. The originality lies in the prompt-based LLM approach for this specific dataset, but the results do not exhibit a sufficient advantage to warrant the significance of this approach. There is no novel prompting strategy developed. They simply used zero, one, and three shot.
*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate. The study demonstrates that LLMs *can* be used for structured data classification in a medical context *without explicit training*. However, the fact that their performance doesn't consistently surpass traditional ML methods on this dataset diminishes the impact. The comparison to traditional models is useful for context, but doesn't reveal any surprises. The observation that prompt engineering impacts performance, and that larger models are generally better, is also not novel. What would have elevated this paper would have been a careful analysis of *why* certain LLMs performed better, and what aspects of the data, or prompt design, contributed to these differences.

    **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive Evaluation: The paper evaluates a range of LLMs and prompting strategies, providing a comparative analysis.
    *   Clear Methodology: The experimental setup is well-defined, and the evaluation metrics are appropriate.
    *   Reproducibility: The code availability enhances the reproducibility of the study.
    *   Comparison with Baselines: The inclusion of traditional ML models provides a useful benchmark for evaluating LLM performance.

    **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited Novelty: The core idea is incremental, and the results are not particularly surprising.
    *   Lack of In-Depth Analysis: The paper lacks a deeper analysis of *why* LLMs perform in the way they do, especially concerning the impact of prompt design and model architecture.
    *   The scope is limited to a single, small dataset (PIDD). Generalizability to other medical datasets is not explored.
    *   The F1-score value is low and therefore the results are not applicable for real-world usage.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper has a useful experimental evaluation, but a lack of significant novelty and lacks deeper insights. The improvement from three-shot prompting is also not consistent and reliable. For these reasons, the current contribution is limited.

**Score: 4**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 4/10

### Thinking in Directivity: Speech Large Language Model for Multi-Talker Directional Speech Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14973v1)
- **Authors**: Jiamin Xie, Ju Lin, Yiteng Huang, Tyler Vuong, Zhaojiang Lin, Zhaojun Yang, Peng Su, Prashant Rawat, Sangeeta Srivastava, Ming Sun, Florian Metze
- **Abstract**: Recent studies have demonstrated that prompting large language models (LLM) with audio encodings enables effective speech recognition capabilities. However, the ability of Speech LLMs to comprehend and process multi-channel audio with spatial cues remains a relatively uninvestigated area of research. In this work, we present directional-SpeechLlama, a novel approach that leverages the microphone array of smart glasses to achieve directional speech recognition, source localization, and bystander cross-talk suppression. To enhance the model's ability to understand directivity, we propose two key techniques: serialized directional output training (S-DOT) and contrastive direction data augmentation (CDDA). Experimental results show that our proposed directional-SpeechLlama effectively captures the relationship between textual cues and spatial audio, yielding strong performance in both speech recognition and source localization tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces directional-SpeechLLaMA, a novel speech large language model (SLLM) designed for multi-channel audio processing, specifically targeting smart glasses applications. The model aims to enhance directional speech recognition, source localization, and cross-talk suppression by leveraging microphone array data. Two key techniques are proposed: Serialized Directional Output Training (S-DOT) and Contrastive Direction Data Augmentation (CDDA).
