These prototypes minimize the nuances of the representation, revealing that seemingly diverse tasks are grounded in shared reasoning structures.Based on this hypothesis, we propose ProtoReasoning, a framework that enhances the reasoning ability of LLMs by leveraging scalable and verifiable prototypical representations (Prolog for logical reasoning, PDDL for planning).ProtoReasoning features: (1) an automated prototype construction pipeline that transforms problems into corresponding prototype representations; (2) a comprehensive verification system providing reliable feedback through Prolog/PDDL interpreters; (3) the scalability to synthesize problems arbitrarily within prototype space while ensuring correctness. Extensive experiments show that ProtoReasoning achieves 4.7% improvement over baseline models on logical reasoning (Enigmata-Eval), 6.3% improvement on planning tasks, 4.0% improvement on general reasoning (MMLU) and 1.0% on mathematics (AIME24). Significantly, our ablation studies confirm that learning in prototype space also demonstrates enhanced generalization to structurally similar problems compared to training solely on natural language representations, validating our hypothesis that reasoning prototypes serve as the foundation for generalizable reasoning in large language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "ProtoReasoning," a framework designed to improve the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) by training them on abstract reasoning prototypes. The central hypothesis is that cross-domain generalization in LLMs arises from shared abstract reasoning patterns across seemingly different tasks. ProtoReasoning uses scalable and verifiable prototypical representations, specifically Prolog for logical reasoning and PDDL for planning. The framework includes an automated prototype construction pipeline, a verification system, and scalability to synthesize problems. Experiments demonstrate improvements on benchmarks like Enigmata-Eval, MMLU, AIME24 and on planning tasks. The ablation studies confirm that learning in the prototype space enhances generalization compared to training solely on natural language. The core claim is that reasoning prototypes act as a foundation for generalizable reasoning in large language models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using abstract reasoning prototypes to enhance LLM reasoning and generalization is interesting and somewhat novel. While the use of Prolog and PDDL in AI is well-established, their application as the *primary training data format* to induce general reasoning *across domains* in LLMs is a relatively fresh approach. The automated pipeline for generating these prototype examples with verification adds to the practical aspect of the method. Previous works have explored Neuro-Symbolic methods, but ProtoReasoning emphasizes formalizing reasoning patterns.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance lies in providing a more principled way to train LLMs for reasoning, potentially leading to more robust and generalizable systems. If proven effective, it could shift the focus from purely data-driven approaches to incorporating more structured, logical representations into the training process. This could be particularly useful in domains where data is scarce or noisy. The results suggesting improvements in various reasoning benchmarks show promise.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Defined Hypothesis:** The paper presents a clear and testable hypothesis about the role of abstract reasoning prototypes in generalization.
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The ProtoReasoning framework is well-structured and includes automated components for prototype generation and verification.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments demonstrate improvements across multiple reasoning benchmarks.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide evidence that training in the prototype space is indeed beneficial for generalization.
    *   Rigorous Verification: using interpreters for verification is a key strength.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The reliance on Prolog and PDDL, while providing strong verification, may limit the applicability of the approach to domains that can be easily represented in these languages. Many reasoning problems may not neatly translate into Prolog/PDDL.
    *   **Dependency on Interpreters:** The verification system's effectiveness is entirely dependent on the quality and completeness of the Prolog/PDDL interpreters. Bugs or limitations in these interpreters could affect the training data quality.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Justification:** While the empirical results are promising, the paper lacks a more formal theoretical explanation for *why* these reasoning prototypes are so effective. Understanding this better could further improve the framework.
    *   **Oversimplification?:** There is a risk that abstracting problems into Prolog/PDDL could discard potentially useful information present in the original natural language form.
    *   The writing style is not very accessible, with overly general statements and a lack of intuitive explanations.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the field by encouraging researchers to explore the use of more structured representations in LLM training. It could also lead to the development of new methods for automatically generating and verifying reasoning problems. The work could also inform the design of more robust and generalizable LLMs.

* **Score Justification**

Considering the factors above, while the research is promising, several aspects limit its impact. The reliance on Prolog/PDDL restricts general applicability, and the work requires more theoretical grounding. However, the framework's construction, verification, and encouraging empirical data make it a valuable contribution.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### LLM vs. SAST: A Technical Analysis on Detecting Coding Bugs of GPT4-Advanced Data Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15212v1)
- **Authors**: Madjid G. Tehrani, Eldar Sultanow, William J. Buchanan, Mahkame Houmani, Christel H. Djaha Fodja
- **Abstract**: With the rapid advancements in Natural Language Processing (NLP), large language models (LLMs) like GPT-4 have gained significant traction in diverse applications, including security vulnerability scanning. This paper investigates the efficacy of GPT-4 in identifying software vulnerabilities compared to traditional Static Application Security Testing (SAST) tools. Drawing from an array of security mistakes, our analysis underscores the potent capabilities of GPT-4 in LLM-enhanced vulnerability scanning. We unveiled that GPT-4 (Advanced Data Analysis) outperforms SAST by an accuracy of 94% in detecting 32 types of exploitable vulnerabilities. This study also addresses the potential security concerns surrounding LLMs, emphasising the imperative of security by design/default and other security best practices for AI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of GPT-4's Advanced Data Analysis in detecting software vulnerabilities compared to traditional Static Application Security Testing (SAST) tools. The authors tested GPT-4 against 32 known security pitfalls, finding that GPT-4 outperformed SAST with a higher accuracy in detecting these vulnerabilities. The paper also addresses potential security concerns surrounding the use of LLMs in security applications, emphasizing the importance of security by design and other best practices.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in empirically evaluating GPT-4's ability to identify vulnerabilities and comparing it directly against existing SAST tools. While the general concept of using LLMs for security tasks isn't entirely new, this work provides a specific, quantitative comparison, contributing a tangible data point to the ongoing discussion. However, the number of SAST tools compared is low (effectively one, as the two tools' results are ORed together). This limits the generalizability of the findings.

**Significance:** The significance of this paper hinges on the potential for LLMs to augment or even replace traditional security tools. If GPT-4 indeed offers superior or comparable performance at a potentially lower cost and faster speed (as the paper suggests), it could have considerable impact on how organizations approach security testing. This could democratize security, making advanced vulnerability detection more accessible. Further, highlighting the dangers of using LLMs for security scanning is critical.  Attackers can use these technologies and there needs to be a defense by design approach.  The paper addresses this point.

**Strengths:**

*   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper provides a concrete, data-driven comparison between GPT-4 and SAST, which is more valuable than purely theoretical discussions.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The methodology is clearly outlined, allowing for reproducibility and further investigation.
*   **Addresses Concerns:** The paper acknowledges the potential risks associated with using LLMs in security, emphasizing the need for responsible development and deployment.
*   **Focus on Practical Application:** The suggestions for future research (broader tool comparisons, usability testing, integration with development environments) are relevant and practical.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited SAST Tool Comparison:** The comparison is limited to effectively one SAST result, obtained by an OR operation of two tools. This limits the ability to extrapolate findings to all SAST solutions.
*   **Lack of Detail on LLM Usage:** The paper mentions using the web interface of GPT-4's Advanced Data Analysis, but further details on the prompting strategies, specific configurations, and potential limitations of this approach are missing. This is critical for reproducibility and for understanding the optimal way to leverage GPT-4 for this task. The paper should contain the exact questions/prompts used to detect security vulnerabilities.
*   **No quantitative measure of LLM-specific security concerns.** Security concerns for LLMs are mentioned, but there is no real measurement or data to support this.
*   **Limited Dataset:** Only 32 vulnerabilities were used to evaluate GPT4 vs SAST.
*  **Cost considerations are not well-defined:** The paper mentions cost as a factor, but there isn't a thorough analysis to back this statement up. There needs to be an evaluation of how much it cost to run the GPT queries.
*   **Moving target**: Since both LLMS and SAST are evolving and progressing daily, our research subject is a moving target. This is a limitation, as stated by the authors.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence security practitioners to explore and evaluate LLMs as complementary tools for vulnerability detection. It could also encourage researchers to further investigate the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in security and to develop more robust and secure methods for their deployment.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the above strengths and weaknesses, the paper merits a score of 6. The empirical evaluation and practical focus contribute positively, but the limited SAST comparison, and lack of detail on LLM-specific security metrics significantly restrict its broader impact. While the findings are interesting, the weaknesses need to be addressed by follow-on work to see if those findings can be better generalized to the field.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### MinosEval: Distinguishing Factoid and Non-Factoid for Tailored Open-Ended QA Evaluation with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15215v1)
- **Authors**: Yongqi Fan, Yating Wang, Guandong Wang, Jie Zhai, Jingping Liu, Qi Ye, Tong Ruan
- **Abstract**: Open-ended question answering (QA) is a key task for evaluating the capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Compared to closed-ended QA, it demands longer answer statements, more nuanced reasoning processes, and diverse expressions, making refined and interpretable automatic evaluation both crucial and challenging. Traditional metrics like ROUGE and BERTScore struggle to capture semantic similarities due to different patterns between model responses and reference answers. Current LLM-based evaluation approaches, such as pairwise or listwise comparisons of candidate answers, lack intuitive interpretability. While pointwise scoring of each response provides some descriptions, it fails to adapt across different question contents. Most notably, existing methods overlook the distinction between factoid and non-factoid questions. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{MinosEval}, a novel evaluation method that first distinguishes open-ended questions and then ranks candidate answers using different evaluation strategies. For factoid questions, it applies an adaptive key-point scoring strategy, while for non-factoid questions, it uses an instance-aware listwise ranking strategy. Experiments on multiple open-ended QA datasets, including self-built ones with more candidate responses to complement community resources, show that MinosEval better aligns with human annotations and offers more interpretable results.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "MinosEval: Distinguishing Factoid and Non-Factoid for Tailored Open-Ended QA Evaluation with LLMs" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MinosEval, a novel two-stage evaluation method for open-ended question answering (QA) designed to improve the accuracy and interpretability of automatic LLM evaluation.  MinosEval differentiates between factoid and non-factoid questions and then applies tailored evaluation strategies. For factoid questions, it uses an adaptive key-point scoring strategy based on natural language inference (NLI). For non-factoid questions, it uses an instance-aware listwise ranking approach, generating "silver" answer instances to aid the LLM in judging quality.  The authors demonstrate its effectiveness on multiple open-ended QA datasets, including two self-built datasets, and show that it aligns better with human annotations than traditional metrics and other LLM-based methods. A key claim is its improved interpretability. The code and datasets will be publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *combined approach* of explicitly distinguishing between question types (factoid vs. non-factoid) and then *applying different evaluation methods specifically tailored to each type*. While LLM-based evaluation isn't new, and neither are key-point extraction or listwise ranking in isolation, the *integration* of these elements with the question type differentiation constitutes a valuable contribution. Other novelty aspect is the creation of two self-built datasets with more candidate responses.

*   **Significance:** Open-ended QA evaluation is a critical challenge in assessing LLM capabilities.  Traditional metrics often fail to capture nuances like reasoning, creativity, and entailment, while existing LLM-based evaluations can lack interpretability and efficiency. By providing a more nuanced, interpretable, and automated evaluation method, MinosEval addresses these limitations. This can lead to a better understanding of LLM strengths and weaknesses, guiding future model development. However, as is noted in the paper, this area of open ended QA evaluation has been extensively researched, with similar approaches being proposed (but without the key distinguishment between factoid and non-factoid).
    The claim of improved interpretability is significant, but it is not shown with human experiments, rather by "case studies."
    The code and datasets are also welcomed contributions.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Tailored evaluation:** The approach of adapting evaluation strategies based on question type is logically sound and empirically validated.
    *   **Improved interpretability:** Key-point scoring and silver answer instances offer insights into the model's reasoning process.
    *   **Automated process:** The entire pipeline is fully automated, removing the need for manual intervention.
    *   **Empirical validation:** Experiments across diverse datasets demonstrate the method's effectiveness.
    *   **Robustness Analysis:** Good to see the standard deviations of the results.
    *   **Datasets:** Complement community resources.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** Like many LLM-based evaluation methods, MinosEval's performance is contingent on the quality and biases of the underlying LLMs (e.g., GPT-4).  The prompts are very important, and these are all in closed-source LLMs. The generalizability of those prompts to other LLMs is uncertain. The paper states the need to re-interpret for greater reliability; indicating that the LLM annotator has some bias.
    *   **Ambiguity in Question Types:** The boundary between factoid and non-factoid questions can be subjective, and misclassification can lead to suboptimal evaluation. Though the experiments demonstrate that it works more often than not. The annotation rules helped reduce this ambiguity.
    *   **Potential for cascading errors:** As the authors themselves note, initial factoid/non-factoid classification errors can propagate through the evaluation pipeline. The impact is discussed, but still present. The table about accuracy of GPT-4 is provided (Table 6).
    *   **Evaluation Metric limitations:** It depends on which evaluation metric is used.
    *   **Limited scale:** Datasets constructed remain limited.
    *   **No ablation of how many silver instances is optimal.**
    *   **No human experiment, only "case studies."**

*   **Potential Influence:** MinosEval has the potential to become a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners developing and evaluating LLMs for open-ended QA tasks. Its improved accuracy and interpretability can facilitate a deeper understanding of LLM performance and guide future research directions. The public availability of the code and datasets will further enhance its impact.

**Score: 7/10**

**Justification:**

MinosEval presents a worthwhile advance in open-ended QA evaluation by strategically adapting evaluation methods to different question types. The method's enhanced interpretability and automated nature are notable strengths. However, its dependence on LLMs, potential for cascading errors, and the subjective nature of the factoid/non-factoid distinction warrant a more conservative assessment. The paper demonstrates promising results, but future work should focus on addressing these limitations and exploring its applicability to an even wider range of datasets and LLMs. The major limitation is the interpretability is provided only through "case studies," not human experiments.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### DM-FNet: Unified multimodal medical image fusion via diffusion process-trained encoder-decoder
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15218v1)
- **Authors**: Dan He, Weisheng Li, Guofen Wang, Yuping Huang, Shiqiang Liu
- **Abstract**: Multimodal medical image fusion (MMIF) extracts the most meaningful information from multiple source images, enabling a more comprehensive and accurate diagnosis. Achieving high-quality fusion results requires a careful balance of brightness, color, contrast, and detail; this ensures that the fused images effectively display relevant anatomical structures and reflect the functional status of the tissues. However, existing MMIF methods have limited capacity to capture detailed features during conventional training and suffer from insufficient cross-modal feature interaction, leading to suboptimal fused image quality. To address these issues, this study proposes a two-stage diffusion model-based fusion network (DM-FNet) to achieve unified MMIF. In Stage I, a diffusion process trains UNet for image reconstruction. UNet captures detailed information through progressive denoising and represents multilevel data, providing a rich set of feature representations for the subsequent fusion network. In Stage II, noisy images at various steps are input into the fusion network to enhance the model's feature recognition capability. Three key fusion modules are also integrated to process medical images from different modalities adaptively. Ultimately, the robust network structure and a hybrid loss function are integrated to harmonize the fused image's brightness, color, contrast, and detail, enhancing its quality and information density. The experimental results across various medical image types demonstrate that the proposed method performs exceptionally well regarding objective evaluation metrics. The fused image preserves appropriate brightness, a comprehensive distribution of radioactive tracers, rich textures, and clear edges. The code is available at https://github.com/HeDan-11/DM-FNet.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DM-FNet: Unified multimodal medical image fusion via diffusion process-trained encoder-decoder" proposes a novel two-stage diffusion model-based network (DM-FNet) for unified multimodal medical image fusion (MMIF).  In Stage I, a UNet is trained using a diffusion process for image reconstruction, capturing detailed features through progressive denoising. In Stage II, a fusion network leverages noisy images from various denoising steps to enhance feature recognition. The fusion network incorporates three key modules: an attention-guided multimodal feature fusion module (AMFF), a multiscale feature fusion module (MSFF) and feature fusion of noised images to adaptively process medical images from different modalities.  A hybrid loss function harmonizes brightness, color, contrast, and detail in the fused image.  The method is evaluated on MRI-CT, MRI-PET, and MRI-SPECT datasets, as well as PC-GFP and IVIF datasets, demonstrating improved performance compared to state-of-the-art techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper introduces several novel elements. The combination of a diffusion model for pre-training an encoder for medical image fusion is a good idea that leverages the strengths of diffusion models in capturing fine details. The two-stage training process with noisy image inputs is novel in this context, and potentially a good way to regularize the fusion network. The attention-guided multimodal feature fusion module and multiscale feature fusion module also show promise, and the design seems to try and remedy the flaws of the state-of-the-art methods. However, diffusion models have already been employed in medical imaging and image fusion, so novelty is incremental.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant problem in medical imaging: improving the quality of fused multimodal images for more accurate diagnosis. If the method consistently and significantly outperforms existing approaches in clinical practice or subsequent work builds upon this to advance the field, it holds significance.  The experimental results suggest improved performance across various image types and metrics, which is a good indicator.  The paper could influence future research directions in medical image fusion, particularly in exploring diffusion models and attention mechanisms.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper evaluates the proposed method on multiple datasets with a wide range of evaluation metrics.
*   **Clear Architecture:**  The architecture of DM-FNet is clearly described, and the functionality of each module is well-explained.
*   **Visual Results:**  The visual comparisons show that DM-FNet produces visually appealing fusion results, preserving details and brightness effectively.
*   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study provides insights into the contribution of each module and the effect of different hyperparameter values.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** Although the approach combines several techniques, the individual components are not entirely novel. The integration is a notable contribution but could have been made more significant with a radical redesign or breakthrough approach.
*   **Computational Complexity:**  While efficient compared to some other diffusion model-based methods, the computational cost of the two-stage training process and diffusion-based approach might still be a limitation for real-time applications, even without the inference sampling stage. The time complexity may not justify the performance gain compared to other less intensive methods. It remains an open question as to whether or not the slight qualitative improvement outweighs the computational cost.
*   **Fine-tuning:** The fine-tuning experiment demonstrates the generalizability of the model, but raises concerns about the need for manual adjustment on new datasets.
*   **Limited Clinical Validation:** The paper focuses on objective metrics and visual results. Clinical validation involving radiologists or other medical experts would strengthen the significance of the work.
*   **Presentation:** There were a few small errors in the formatting of the table where ranking for the different metrics were reported. This distracts from the otherwise carefully constructed paper.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of medical image fusion by introducing a novel architecture based on diffusion models. However, the novelty is incremental rather than transformative, and the clinical significance is not fully established. While the experimental results are promising, further validation and exploration of computational efficiency are needed. I would assign a score of **7** out of 10, reflecting the paper's good technical quality and potential impact, but also acknowledging its limitations in novelty and clinical relevance.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### video-SALMONN 2: Captioning-Enhanced Audio-Visual Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15220v1)
- **Authors**: Changli Tang, Yixuan Li, Yudong Yang, Jimin Zhuang, Guangzhi Sun, Wei Li, Zejun Ma, Chao Zhang
- **Abstract**: Videos contain a wealth of information, and generating detailed and accurate descriptions in natural language is a key aspect of video understanding. In this paper, we present video-SALMONN 2, an advanced audio-visual large language model (LLM) with low-rank adaptation (LoRA) designed for enhanced video (with paired audio) captioning through directed preference optimisation (DPO). We propose new metrics to evaluate the completeness and accuracy of video descriptions, which are optimised using DPO. To further improve training, we propose a novel multi-round DPO (MrDPO) approach, which involves periodically updating the DPO reference model, merging and re-initialising the LoRA module as a proxy for parameter updates after each training round (1,000 steps), and incorporating guidance from ground-truth video captions to stabilise the process. Experimental results show that MrDPO significantly enhances video-SALMONN 2's captioning accuracy, reducing the captioning error rates by 28\%. The final video-SALMONN 2 model, with just 7 billion parameters, surpasses leading models such as GPT-4o and Gemini-1.5-Pro in video captioning tasks, while maintaining highly competitive performance to the state-of-the-art on widely used video question-answering benchmarks among models of similar size. Codes are available at \href{https://github.com/bytedance/video-SALMONN-2}{https://github.com/bytedance/video-SALMONN-2}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces video-SALMONN 2, an audio-visual large language model (LLM) designed for improved video captioning, especially when paired with audio.  It utilizes low-rank adaptation (LoRA) and a novel multi-round direct preference optimization (MrDPO) approach. MrDPO refines the model's captioning capabilities using new metrics for completeness and accuracy.  Key innovations include periodic updates to the DPO reference model, merging LoRA modules, and ground-truth caption guidance. Experiments demonstrate that video-SALMONN 2, with only 7 billion parameters, outperforms larger commercial models like GPT-40 and Gemini-1.5-Pro in video captioning tasks, while remaining competitive on video question-answering benchmarks.  The code is made available.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the MrDPO method tailored for video captioning. Updating the DPO reference model periodically, merging LoRA modules, and using ground-truth caption guidance seems like a practical solution to the problem of reference model bias and instability in DPO training. The introduction of new metrics for video caption completeness and accuracy using another LLM as a judge is also a meaningful contribution, especially given the limitations of standard metrics like BLEU and ROUGE. However, it's important to acknowledge that DPO itself is not a new technique, and LoRA has been around for a while. The specific combination of these techniques within the context of audio-visual video captioning, along with the proposed modifications, contributes to the paper's overall novelty.

*   **Significance:** Video captioning is a fundamental task for video understanding, and improving the quality of captions has broad implications. The fact that a relatively small model (7B parameters) can outperform larger commercial models is significant from a resource efficiency perspective. Making the code available will promote further research and development in this area. The introduction of new evaluation metrics also has the potential to impact how video captioning models are assessed. However, the paper focuses primarily on captioning. The model's performance in other areas of multimodal understanding might be limited, which could restrict its broader impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and well-motivated approach.
    *   Detailed description of the MrDPO method.
    *   Quantitative evaluation demonstrating significant improvements over existing models, including commercial ones.
    *   Introduction of new evaluation metrics for video caption quality.
    *   Code availability.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The evaluation focuses heavily on video captioning. It would be useful to see the model's performance on a broader range of tasks, especially in audio-visual reasoning.
    *   The dependence on a powerful LLM (GPT-3.5 or GPT-4) for evaluation could introduce bias. An alternative evaluation method with human annotators could be a valuable addition. Also, the evaluation method uses the LLM to determine if a caption is "hallucinating," which can be seen as subjective since LLMs also generate opinions. The paper attempts to mitigate the LLM's opinions from ground truth data as hallucination, but they are not completely removed.
    *   The paper mentions some slight regression in video QA.
    *   The model makes heavy use of off-the-shelf components, limiting novelty.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work can influence future research by providing a strong baseline for video captioning and demonstrating the effectiveness of MrDPO. The evaluation metrics can be adopted by other researchers to better assess the quality of their models.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of audio-visual video captioning. The MrDPO method shows promising results and the introduction of new evaluation metrics addresses an important gap in the literature. However, the reliance on established techniques (LoRA, DPO) and the limited scope of the evaluation prevents a higher score. The fact that the code is available greatly increases the potential impact. The score reflects a solid and impactful contribution but not a groundbreaking advancement.
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models for Unit Testing: A Systematic Literature Review
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15227v1)
- **Authors**: Quanjun Zhang, Chunrong Fang, Siqi Gu, Ye Shang, Zhenyu Chen, Liang Xiao
- **Abstract**: Unit testing is a fundamental practice in modern software engineering, with the aim of ensuring the correctness, maintainability, and reliability of individual software components. Very recently, with the advances in Large Language Models (LLMs), a rapidly growing body of research has leveraged LLMs to automate various unit testing tasks, demonstrating remarkable performance and significantly reducing manual effort. However, due to ongoing explorations in the LLM-based unit testing field, it is challenging for researchers to understand existing achievements, open challenges, and future opportunities. This paper presents the first systematic literature review on the application of LLMs in unit testing until March 2025. We analyze \numpaper{} relevant papers from the perspectives of both unit testing and LLMs. We first categorize existing unit testing tasks that benefit from LLMs, e.g., test generation and oracle generation. We then discuss several critical aspects of integrating LLMs into unit testing research, including model usage, adaptation strategies, and hybrid approaches. We further summarize key challenges that remain unresolved and outline promising directions to guide future research in this area. Overall, our paper provides a systematic overview of the research landscape to the unit testing community, helping researchers gain a comprehensive understanding of achievements and promote future research. Our artifacts are publicly available at the GitHub repository: https://github.com/iSEngLab/AwesomeLLM4UT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a systematic literature review (SLR) of the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) in unit testing.  It analyzes 105 relevant papers published until March 2025, categorizing them based on unit testing tasks benefiting from LLMs (e.g., test generation, oracle generation) and aspects of LLM integration (model usage, adaptation strategies).  The review also identifies key challenges and promising directions for future research in this area.  The paper aims to provide a comprehensive overview of the research landscape to the unit testing community, aiding understanding and promoting future research.  The authors provide access to their curated artifact collection on GitHub.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper claims to be the first SLR specifically focusing on LLMs for unit testing. This claim is plausible, as existing surveys focus on either general LLM applications in software engineering or traditional unit testing techniques *before* the LLM boom.  The timing is significant. While earlier surveys might have touched upon the *potential* of LLMs, this paper analyzes *actual applications* with concrete results. The novelty comes from synthesising a fast-growing area in a focused domain.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in providing a much-needed overview of a rapidly evolving field. The SLR format allows for a structured analysis, identifying trends, open problems, and potential future directions that would be difficult to grasp without such a synthesis. The categorizations (UT perspective, LLM perspective, model usage strategies) are valuable for researchers seeking to navigate the existing literature. A GitHub repository containing the papers, facilitates reproducibility and further exploration by the community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   *Comprehensive Scope:* The review analyzes a substantial number of papers (105), providing a broad view of the field.
    *   *Structured Analysis:* The well-defined categories (UT tasks, LLM usage) facilitate a systematic understanding.
    *   *Identified Challenges and Opportunities:* Highlighting limitations and future directions is crucial for guiding further research.
    *   *Open Artifacts:*  Availability of the paper collection adds significant value for the community.
    *   *Timeliness:* Publication before the end of the period reviewed is important for capturing the latest findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Depth vs. Breadth:*  While the breadth is a strength, there might be a trade-off in the depth of analysis for each individual paper. Some areas (e.g. hybrid methods of traditional Software Engineering with LLMs), lack the nuanced discussion necessary to fully understand the synergistic potential.
    *   *Potential Bias in Selection:*  The search strategy relies on specific keywords and venues, potentially missing relevant work from less-known sources.  The inclusion/exclusion criteria and the quality assessment step attempt to address this, but some level of bias is inherent.
    *   *The "Moving Target" Problem:*  The field of LLMs is evolving so rapidly that some aspects of the review (e.g., specific model performance) might become outdated quickly. The models listed are already beginning to show their age.
    *   *Lack of practical impact assessment.* The survey focuses on *academic* papers without a clear distinction on industrial or real-world deployments. The survey would have been strengthened through understanding industrial considerations/ deployments (e.g. through interviewing industry adopters)

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has strong potential to influence the field by providing a foundation for future research and development. It helps researchers:

    *   Understand the current state of the art.
    *   Identify promising research directions.
    *   Avoid redundant work.
    *   Compare and benchmark different approaches.

The survey successfully identifies both strengths and weaknesses across a fast growing research area, while pointing towards clear opportunities for further development. However, the paper neglects to understand the industrial implications and considerations.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper is a valuable contribution to the field of LLM-based unit testing due to its comprehensive scope, structured analysis, identification of challenges and opportunities, and the open availability of artifacts. While it has some limitations in depth, potential bias, and rapid technological obsolescence, its benefits outweigh these drawbacks, making it a significant resource for researchers in this area. The high impact score is driven by the strong need for research in this area, and this paper being the primary source of structured data about it. The overall score is brought down due to a relatively shallow level of analysis in various topics, and the lack of understanding of industrial implications.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Lost in Variation? Evaluating NLI Performance in Basque and Spanish Geographical Variants
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15239v1)
- **Authors**: Jaione Bengoetxea, Itziar Gonzalez-Dios, Rodrigo Agerri
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we evaluate the capacity of current language technologies to understand Basque and Spanish language varieties. We use Natural Language Inference (NLI) as a pivot task and introduce a novel, manually-curated parallel dataset in Basque and Spanish, along with their respective variants. Our empirical analysis of crosslingual and in-context learning experiments using encoder-only and decoder-based Large Language Models (LLMs) shows a performance drop when handling linguistic variation, especially in Basque. Error analysis suggests that this decline is not due to lexical overlap, but rather to the linguistic variation itself. Further ablation experiments indicate that encoder-only models particularly struggle with Western Basque, which aligns with linguistic theory that identifies peripheral dialects (e.g., Western) as more distant from the standard. All data and code are publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Lost in Variation? Evaluating NLI Performance in Basque and Spanish Geographical Variants":

**Summary:**

This paper explores how well current language models (LLMs) handle geographical variation in Basque and Spanish. The authors introduce XNLIvar, a new, manually curated parallel dataset for Natural Language Inference (NLI) tasks in both languages, featuring standard forms alongside dialectal variations. Through experiments using encoder-only and decoder-based LLMs, they demonstrate that performance drops significantly when processing dialectal variations, especially in Basque. Their analysis suggests that this decrease isn't primarily due to lexical overlap but rather stems from the linguistic differences themselves. Ablation experiments further indicate that encoder-only models particularly struggle with Western Basque, aligning with linguistic theory identifying peripheral dialects as more distant from standard forms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and important issue: the ability of NLP systems to handle linguistic variation.  The need for NLP systems to be more inclusive and less biased toward standard languages is increasingly recognized. The authors focus on Basque and Spanish, which, while not entirely novel (some prior work exists), provides a valuable contribution due to the manual curation and detailed dialectal distinctions.

*   **Novelty:**
    *   **Dataset:** The creation of XNLIvar is a significant contribution.  Manually curated datasets that are dialect-aware are rare, especially for lower-resource languages like Basque.  The inclusion of speaker metadata adds sociolinguistic value.  However, the relatively small size of the dataset (compared to massive pre-training datasets) and the limited number of speakers contributing the variants are limitations.
    *   **Analysis:** The systematic evaluation using various LLMs and NLI tasks is well-executed.  The error analysis, focusing on lexical overlap and linguistic distance, provides insightful observations. The ablation studies aimed at understanding specific dialect performance is also insightful. The focus on Western Basque is compelling.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Impact:** The findings have implications for NLP model development, particularly concerning bias and fairness.  The demonstrated performance drop highlights the importance of considering linguistic variation in model training and evaluation. This work also opens the door for more detailed sociolinguistic research using NLP tools.
    *   **Limitations:**  While the paper carefully acknowledges the limited number of contributing speakers and dataset size, these are significant limitations that impact the generalizability of the findings. The dataset leans significantly more towards Central Basque, so findings there should be caveated. Further work needs to expand on the data to test these preliminary findings robustly.

*   **Justification of Score:**
    The paper makes a valuable contribution by shedding light on the challenges LLMs face when confronted with linguistic variation, particularly in the context of Basque and Spanish. The XNLIvar dataset, while small, is a significant asset, and the thorough experimental analysis provides valuable insights. However, the study's limitations in dataset size, speaker representation, and the exclusive focus on NLI somewhat constrain its immediate impact.

Score: 7

**Rationale:**

*   **+2 points** for the novel XNLIvar dataset and its detailed annotation.
*   **+2 points** for its focus on Basque, a low-resource language often neglected in variation studies.
*   **+1 points** for the rigorous experimental design and error analysis, specifically relating dialectal distance to performance and focusing on Western Basque.
*   **+1 point** for addressing a growing concern in the field: bias and inclusivity in language technology.
*   **+1 point** for the effort made to involve speakers to capture the nuances of language variation
*   **-2 point** for the small dataset size, which is a significant limitation affecting the generalizability of the conclusions
*   **-1 point** for the limited diversity of speaker backgrounds (all being linguists or NLP experts) and focus on NLI alone as the evaluation task.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Research on Graph-Retrieval Augmented Generation Based on Historical Text Knowledge Graphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15241v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Fan, Zhang Qi, Xing Wenqian, Liu Chang, Liu Liu
- **Abstract**: This article addresses domain knowledge gaps in general large language models for historical text analysis in the context of computational humanities and AIGC technology. We propose the Graph RAG framework, combining chain-of-thought prompting, self-instruction generation, and process supervision to create a The First Four Histories character relationship dataset with minimal manual annotation. This dataset supports automated historical knowledge extraction, reducing labor costs. In the graph-augmented generation phase, we introduce a collaborative mechanism between knowledge graphs and retrieval-augmented generation, improving the alignment of general models with historical knowledge. Experiments show that the domain-specific model Xunzi-Qwen1.5-14B, with Simplified Chinese input and chain-of-thought prompting, achieves optimal performance in relation extraction (F1 = 0.68). The DeepSeek model integrated with GraphRAG improves F1 by 11% (0.08-0.19) on the open-domain C-CLUE relation extraction dataset, surpassing the F1 value of Xunzi-Qwen1.5-14B (0.12), effectively alleviating hallucinations phenomenon, and improving interpretability. This framework offers a low-resource solution for classical text knowledge extraction, advancing historical knowledge services and humanities research.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Research on Graph-Retrieval Augmented Generation Based on Historical Text Knowledge Graphs".

**Summary:**

This paper proposes a GraphRAG framework to address the limitations of general large language models (LLMs) when analyzing historical Chinese texts, particularly regarding knowledge extraction. The framework combines chain-of-thought prompting, self-instruction generation, and process supervision to create a character relationship dataset ("The First Four Histories"). This dataset is then used to train a domain-specific LLM, and the resulting system uses GraphRAG to improve knowledge retrieval and generation. The paper demonstrates that this approach enhances relation extraction performance, reduces "hallucinations," and improves interpretability. The authors show improvements in F1 scores with the domain-specific model and with the GraphRAG-enhanced general model. They conclude that their framework offers a low-resource solution for classical text knowledge extraction and promotes the digital transformation of historical knowledge reconstruction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of several existing techniques—GraphRAG, chain-of-thought prompting, self-instruction, and process supervision—into a cohesive framework for historical text analysis is a key contribution. While the individual components are not entirely new, their integrated application to this specific domain, along with the creation of a new dataset, suggests some level of novelty. The entity expansion retrieval optimization strategies further enhance the application, which are tailored to address the sparsity and entity ambiguity of historical texts.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a relevant problem: the difficulty of applying general LLMs to specialized historical texts due to knowledge gaps and the potential for inaccuracies (hallucinations). The proposed GraphRAG framework offers a practical approach to improve the performance and reliability of LLMs in this domain. The emphasis on a low-resource solution is also valuable, as it addresses the challenges of working with limited annotated data.

    **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper provides a well-defined framework that addresses both knowledge graph construction and retrieval-augmented generation.
    *   **Detailed Experiments:** The paper includes a thorough experimental evaluation, comparing different prompt strategies, model types, and input glyphs.
    *   **Performance Improvements:** The experimental results demonstrate tangible improvements in relation extraction accuracy and hallucination reduction.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers a valuable tool and methodology for researchers and practitioners working with historical texts and other specialized domains.

    **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses primarily on the "The First Four Histories," which, although a significant corpus, might not fully represent the diversity of all historical texts. Generalizability to other genres within Chinese history is a concern that needs further validation.
    *   **Computational Resources:** The authors acknowledge that the domain-specific models require substantial computational resources, potentially limiting their accessibility to some researchers. While the framework aims to be low-resource overall, the initial training/fine-tuning phase still requires significant investment.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the paper shows improvements, it's important to acknowledge that the improvements, especially on the open-domain dataset are moderate rather than transformative. The overall contribution is solid, but perhaps not ground-breaking.
    *   **Lack of Ablation Study**: Although the work integrates a multitude of components, like entity expansion, a thorough ablation study testing each component will better demonstrate the contributions and significance.

    **Overall:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing the challenges of applying LLMs to historical text analysis. While the individual components of the framework are not entirely novel, their integrated application and the creation of a new dataset demonstrate a clear advance in the field. The experimental results are promising, and the emphasis on a low-resource solution enhances the practical value of the work.

Score: 7.5

**Justification for the score of 7.5:** The integration of existing techniques to address a specific domain challenge, coupled with experimental validation and creation of a specific dataset, provides sufficient significance. The identified weaknesses (scope limitation, computational resources, incremental improvement) prevent a higher score. The paper is a strong contribution, providing a practical and effective method for historical text analysis using LLMs. The thorough evaluations and useful combination of various methods enhance the impact of the framework.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unlocking Post-hoc Dataset Inference with Synthetic Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15271v1)
- **Authors**: Bihe Zhao, Pratyush Maini, Franziska Boenisch, Adam Dziedzic
- **Abstract**: The remarkable capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) can be mainly attributed to their massive training datasets, which are often scraped from the internet without respecting data owners' intellectual property rights. Dataset Inference (DI) offers a potential remedy by identifying whether a suspect dataset was used in training, thereby enabling data owners to verify unauthorized use. However, existing DI methods require a private set-known to be absent from training-that closely matches the compromised dataset's distribution. Such in-distribution, held-out data is rarely available in practice, severely limiting the applicability of DI. In this work, we address this challenge by synthetically generating the required held-out set. Our approach tackles two key obstacles: (1) creating high-quality, diverse synthetic data that accurately reflects the original distribution, which we achieve via a data generator trained on a carefully designed suffix-based completion task, and (2) bridging likelihood gaps between real and synthetic data, which is realized through post-hoc calibration. Extensive experiments on diverse text datasets show that using our generated data as a held-out set enables DI to detect the original training sets with high confidence, while maintaining a low false positive rate. This result empowers copyright owners to make legitimate claims on data usage and demonstrates our method's reliability for real-world litigations. Our code is available at https://github.com/sprintml/PostHocDatasetInference.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of current Dataset Inference (DI) methods, which require a private, in-distribution held-out dataset. Such datasets are rarely available in practice, hindering the application of DI for verifying unauthorized use of data in training Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors propose a novel approach to synthetically generate the required held-out data. This is achieved by:

1.  Training a data generator on the suspect dataset using a carefully designed suffix-based completion task to create high-quality, diverse synthetic data.
2.  Introducing a post-hoc calibration step using a dual-classifier approach to bridge the likelihood gaps between real and synthetic data and disentangle the effects of distributional shifts from genuine membership signals.

The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed method on diverse text datasets, showing high confidence in detecting original training sets while maintaining a low false positive rate. This empowers copyright owners to make legitimate claims on data usage.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using *synthetic* data generation to overcome the held-out data requirement of DI is innovative. Previous work relied on real held-out datasets, making the method vulnerable to subtle distributional shifts or outright impracticality. The specific techniques for generating this data (suffix completion) and calibrating for differences between real and synthetic data are also novel contributions. The dual-classifier approach to disentangling distributional artifacts from membership signals is a clever methodological contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance is high. The ability to perform DI without reliance on real, in-distribution held-out data greatly expands the applicability of DI for copyright protection and auditing data usage in LLM training. Given the ongoing concerns about data scraping and unauthorized use of copyrighted material, this work offers a practical solution. The demonstrated low false positive rate is critical for the method's credibility and trustworthiness in real-world legal or auditing scenarios. The results on real datasets further substantiate the claim that this technique has the potential to make data privacy in LLMs more accessible.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The problem addressed is highly relevant and timely.
    *   The proposed solution is technically sound and well-motivated.
    *   The synthetic data generation and calibration techniques are novel.
    *   The experimental evaluation is thorough, covering a range of datasets and model sizes.
    *   The paper carefully analyzes and addresses potential limitations (distributional shifts).
    *   The work offers a pathway to safeguard intellectual property in the age of large language models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    * While the approach is effective, the implementation can be complex, requiring careful design of the generator and calibration steps. The hyperparameter sensitivity needs further exploring for practical applicability.
    * There might be concerns about the extent to which synthetic data can truly capture all aspects of a real dataset's distribution. Are there edge cases where the synthetic data generation fails, leading to inaccurate DI? More discussion of limitations of synthetic data generation is warranted.
    * While the paper explores different text classifier architectures, a more detailed analysis of how the choice of classifier impacts performance would be valuable.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in several areas:
    *   Dataset Inference: The synthetic data generation approach could become a standard technique for DI.
    *   Copyright Protection: The work can have an impact on how copyright is enforced in the age of LLMs.
    *   Data Privacy Auditing: The method can be used to audit LLMs for unauthorized data usage.
    *   Generative Modeling: The suffix-based completion task might be a useful technique for other generative modeling applications.

*   **Overall:** The work represents a significant advancement in the field of dataset inference, addressing a crucial limitation and offering a practical solution with compelling experimental evidence. While some further refinement and deeper discussion of the limitations would be beneficial, the novelty and significance are undeniable.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Human Motion Capture from Loose and Sparse Inertial Sensors with Garment-aware Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15290v1)
- **Authors**: Andela Ilic, Jiaxi Jiang, Paul Streli, Xintong Liu, Christian Holz
- **Abstract**: Motion capture using sparse inertial sensors has shown great promise due to its portability and lack of occlusion issues compared to camera-based tracking. Existing approaches typically assume that IMU sensors are tightly attached to the human body. However, this assumption often does not hold in real-world scenarios. In this paper, we present a new task of full-body human pose estimation using sparse, loosely attached IMU sensors. To solve this task, we simulate IMU recordings from an existing garment-aware human motion dataset. We developed transformer-based diffusion models to synthesize loose IMU data and estimate human poses based on this challenging loose IMU data. In addition, we show that incorporating garment-related parameters while training the model on simulated loose data effectively maintains expressiveness and enhances the ability to capture variations introduced by looser or tighter garments. Experiments show that our proposed diffusion methods trained on simulated and synthetic data outperformed the state-of-the-art methods quantitatively and qualitatively, opening up a promising direction for future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of full-body human motion capture using sparse inertial measurement units (IMUs) that are loosely attached to clothing. This is a more realistic scenario than assuming tightly fitted IMUs, which is common in prior work. The authors propose a novel approach based on conditional diffusion models trained on a combination of simulated, synthetic, and real-world data. They simulate loose IMU data from existing garment-aware human motion datasets and use a secondary diffusion model to generate more realistic synthetic data. A key contribution is the incorporation of garment-related parameters (height, BMI, garment style) into the training process to improve pose estimation accuracy and robustness to variations in garment fit. The paper presents experimental results on several datasets (AMASS, LIP, a real-world whole-body dataset) demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed method compared to state-of-the-art approaches.  The authors also conduct ablation studies to analyze the contribution of various components of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:

    *   **Problem Formulation:** Explicitly tackling full-body pose estimation from sparse, *loosely attached* IMUs is a valuable and practically relevant contribution. While previous work (LIP) addressed loosely attached IMUs, it focused on upper-body estimation and didn't thoroughly explore the influence of garment-related parameters on full-body estimation.
    *   **Data Generation Strategy:** The multi-source training strategy, combining simulated, synthetic (diffusion-generated), and real-world data, is a powerful approach to bridge the gap between synthetic and real-world data and improve generalization.
    *   **Garment-Awareness:** Incorporating garment-related parameters is a significant advancement that distinguishes this work from prior approaches that often treat garment effects as simply added noise. The authors provide empirical evidence of its effectiveness.
    *   **Diffusion Model Application:**  While diffusion models are increasingly used in motion generation, their application in this specific problem domain, combined with the novel data synthesis techniques, is novel.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to improve the practicality and usability of IMU-based motion capture. By relaxing the tight-fitting requirement, the technology becomes more accessible and comfortable for users. The demonstrated improvements in pose estimation accuracy, especially under varying garment conditions, are significant. This could have a substantial impact on applications like virtual reality, rehabilitation, sports analysis, and other areas where accurate and unobtrusive motion capture is needed.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes extensive experiments on multiple datasets with different characteristics, demonstrating the robustness of the proposed method.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of different components of the approach (e.g., the consistency regularization loss, conditioning on specific parameters).
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and experimental setup.
    *   **Addressing Limitations of Prior Work:** The authors clearly identify and address the limitations of existing approaches.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Real-World Garment Parameter Estimation:**  A major limitation is that garment parameters (height, BMI, garment style) are manually selected/inferred, not automatically estimated from the IMU data or other sensors. This limits the practicality of the approach in truly uncontrolled settings. If the method relies on being manually specified these values at test time it reduces the impact of the work. It would be more significant if the authors could estimate these garment parameters alongside the pose.
    *   **Dataset Limitations:**  While the authors use a variety of datasets, they acknowledge that existing datasets may not fully capture the diversity of real-world garment configurations and human body shapes. Further real-world data collection efforts focusing on a wide range of body shapes and clothing types would benefit the field.
    *   **Diffusion Model Complexity:** Diffusion models, while powerful, can be computationally expensive.  The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the real-time performance and computational requirements of the proposed method.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of IMU-based motion capture by addressing a key practical limitation (tight-fitting sensors). The novel data generation and garment-aware training strategies are likely to inspire future research in this area.  The paper opens up several avenues for future work, including exploring methods for automatic garment parameter estimation, improving real-time performance, and addressing the limitations of existing datasets.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a significant advancement in IMU-based motion capture by addressing the practical challenge of loose-fitting sensors and the impact of garment variations. The multi-source training strategy, garment-aware approach, and thorough evaluation are strong points. However, the manual estimation of garment parameters and potential computational cost represent limitations. Given its significant novelty and potential impact, balanced against these weaknesses, a score of 7 is warranted. The paper moves the field forward in a meaningful way, but further research is needed to fully realize its potential and address existing limitations.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### MEGC2025: Micro-Expression Grand Challenge on Spot Then Recognize and Visual Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15298v1)
- **Authors**: Xinqi Fan, Jingting Li, John See, Moi Hoon Yap, Wen-Huang Cheng, Xiaobai Li, Xiaopeng Hong, Su-Jing Wang, Adrian K. Davision
- **Abstract**: Facial micro-expressions (MEs) are involuntary movements of the face that occur spontaneously when a person experiences an emotion but attempts to suppress or repress the facial expression, typically found in a high-stakes environment. In recent years, substantial advancements have been made in the areas of ME recognition, spotting, and generation. However, conventional approaches that treat spotting and recognition as separate tasks are suboptimal, particularly for analyzing long-duration videos in realistic settings. Concurrently, the emergence of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and large vision-language models (LVLMs) offers promising new avenues for enhancing ME analysis through their powerful multimodal reasoning capabilities. The ME grand challenge (MEGC) 2025 introduces two tasks that reflect these evolving research directions: (1) ME spot-then-recognize (ME-STR), which integrates ME spotting and subsequent recognition in a unified sequential pipeline; and (2) ME visual question answering (ME-VQA), which explores ME understanding through visual question answering, leveraging MLLMs or LVLMs to address diverse question types related to MEs. All participating algorithms are required to run on this test set and submit their results on a leaderboard. More details are available at https://megc2025.github.io.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the MEGC2025, a grand challenge focusing on two tasks related to micro-expression analysis: (1) ME Spot-Then-Recognize (ME-STR), which combines ME spotting and recognition into a single pipeline, and (2) ME Visual Question Answering (ME-VQA), which utilizes multimodal large language models (MLLMs) or large vision-language models (LVLMs) to understand MEs through visual question answering. The ME-STR task builds upon previous challenges by integrating spotting and recognition, addressing limitations of treating them separately. The ME-VQA task is novel, leveraging the capabilities of MLLMs/LVLMs to answer questions about MEs in natural language, promoting interpretability and human-aligned interaction. The paper details the datasets used for evaluation, baseline methods (MEAN for ME-STR and Qwen2.5VL-3B for ME-VQA), and evaluation metrics used. Preliminary results on both tasks are provided, highlighting the challenges and providing a starting point for challenge participants.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the ME-VQA task. While ME-STR is a continuation of a previous challenge, ME-VQA represents a new direction by leveraging MLLMs/LVLMs for ME understanding and employing a question-answering paradigm. This explores a different way of interpreting and analyzing MEs. The novelty of the ME-STR task is incremental, improving upon the 2024 challenge.

*   **Significance:** The ME-VQA task has significant potential. By reframing ME analysis as a question-answering problem, the authors aim to increase interpretability and enable more flexible interactions with ME data. This approach could pave the way for human-centered applications, such as automated emotion recognition in human-computer interaction.  The ME-STR contributes to a more realistic ME analysis pipeline.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly defines both tasks and motivates their importance in the context of ME research.
    *   **Leveraging recent advances:** The ME-VQA task effectively utilizes the emerging capabilities of MLLMs/LVLMs to address challenges in ME analysis.
    *   **Realistic task setting:** The ME-STR task adopts a more realistic approach by integrating spotting and recognition.
    *   **Well-defined evaluation metrics:** The paper uses appropriate evaluation metrics to assess the performance of both tasks, ensuring a fair comparison between different algorithms.
    *   **Baseline results:** Providing baseline results allows for a more objective evaluation of submissions for the challenges.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited experimental results:** The paper presents only preliminary results with a baseline model. More extensive experiments and analysis could strengthen the paper. The STR task result section would be more compelling with results for all challenges and not just the presented baseline.
    *   **Dataset Limitations:** Although the paper recommends existing datasets, it acknowledges that a truly large-scale, spontaneous ME dataset is still lacking. The test datasets are relatively small, which may limit the generalizability of the challenge results.
    *   **Lack of in-depth analysis for VQA Baseline:** Further analysis of the VQA baseline results is needed to determine the specific strengths and weaknesses of the approach. How is the performance of the models when asked different question types, for example.
    *   **Modest contribution from STR task:** The STR task is a continuation of a previous challenge, with limited novelty.

*   **Impact:** The MEGC challenges have historically played a significant role in advancing ME research. This MEGC 2025 has the potential to impact research by focusing research and progress on VQA in ME analysis and on more integrated spot-then-recognize frameworks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of micro-expression analysis, particularly through its introduction of the ME-VQA task, which leverages recent advances in MLLMs/LVLMs. The ME-STR task also is an important step to a unified and realistic ME analysis. The well-defined tasks, datasets, and evaluation metrics, coupled with the challenge format, are expected to motivate researchers and drive progress in the field. The weaknesses in the experimental results and the limitations of the data however limit the overall impact of the contribution.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### SecFwT: Efficient Privacy-Preserving Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models Using Forward-Only Passes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15307v1)
- **Authors**: Jinglong Luo, Zhuo Zhang, Yehong Zhang, Shiyu Liu, Ye Dong, Xun Zhou, Hui Wang, Yue Yu, Zenglin Xu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have transformed numerous fields, yet their adaptation to specialized tasks in privacy-sensitive domains, such as healthcare and finance, is constrained by the scarcity of accessible training data due to stringent privacy requirements. Secure multi-party computation (MPC)-based privacy-preserving machine learning offers a powerful approach to protect both model parameters and user data, but its application to LLMs has been largely limited to inference, as fine-tuning introduces significant computational challenges, particularly in privacy-preserving backward propagation and optimizer operations. This paper identifies two primary obstacles to MPC-based privacy-preserving fine-tuning of LLMs: (1) the substantial computational overhead of backward and optimizer processes, and (2) the inefficiency of softmax-based attention mechanisms in MPC settings. To address these challenges, we propose SecFwT, the first MPC-based framework designed for efficient, privacy-preserving LLM fine-tuning. SecFwT introduces a forward-only tuning paradigm to eliminate backward and optimizer computations and employs MPC-friendly Random Feature Attention to approximate softmax attention, significantly reducing costly non-linear operations and computational complexity. Experimental results demonstrate that SecFwT delivers substantial improvements in efficiency and privacy preservation, enabling scalable and secure fine-tuning of LLMs for privacy-critical applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "SECFWT: Efficient Privacy-Preserving Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models Using Forward-Only Passes" introduces a novel framework for privacy-preserving fine-tuning (PPFT) of Large Language Models (LLMs) using Secure Multi-Party Computation (MPC).  The framework, named SECFWT, addresses the computational challenges of applying MPC to LLM fine-tuning by: (1) employing a forward-only tuning (FoT) paradigm that eliminates the need for backpropagation and optimizer computations, and (2) using MPC-friendly Random Feature Attention (RFA) to approximate softmax attention, thereby reducing costly non-linear operations. The authors demonstrate through experiments that SECFWT achieves significant improvements in efficiency (time and communication) compared to baseline PPFT methods, with minimal performance degradation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a significant contribution by being among the first to address the challenging problem of *efficient* privacy-preserving *fine-tuning* of LLMs using MPC. While other works focus on inference or limit fine-tuning to smaller components, SECFWT provides a more comprehensive solution.  The core novelty lies in the combination of forward-only tuning and MPC-friendly RFA specifically optimized for MPC environments. The use of gradient-free optimization within MPC for fine-tuning, though inspired by BBT, is tailored and optimized for the secure computation setting. The RFA adaptation with trigonometric optimizations is also a novel element. However, it's important to note that FoT and RFA themselves are not entirely new ideas, but their clever combination and MPC-specific optimization within this context represents a novel approach.
* **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to unlock privacy-sensitive applications of LLMs in domains like healthcare and finance. Enabling efficient PPFT allows for the customization of powerful models to specific tasks while protecting user data and model parameters. The experimental results clearly demonstrate the practical benefits, showing substantial speedups and reduced communication costs.  The work provides a crucial step towards making PPML for LLMs a viable option in real-world settings.  The framework's contribution to enabling collaborative development of specialized LLMs without compromising data privacy is a key benefit. A weakness is that the evaluation is currently limited to ROBERTa large, but the results are convincing.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the bottlenecks in applying MPC to LLM fine-tuning.
    * **Well-Designed Framework:** The SECFWT framework is well-structured and combines FoT and RFA effectively.
    * **MPC-Specific Optimizations:** The authors tailor the algorithms for MPC, addressing the specific challenges of secure computation.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides strong experimental evidence of efficiency improvements.
    * **Comprehensive approach:** Focuses on both forward passes and attention mechanisms.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Model Evaluation:** The evaluation is primarily focused on ROBERTa large, which might not fully generalize to other LLM architectures. Expanding the experiments to GPT-style models or other architectures would strengthen the paper.
    * **Performance Trade-off:** While the performance degradation is reported as "slight," a more in-depth analysis of the impact on downstream task accuracy would be beneficial.  Also, how does the optimization iteration of GFO in SECFWT affects the generalization capability of LLMs.
    * **Limited novelty of core methods:** FoT and RFA are not new concepts. The novelty is in the optimized application to PPFT.

* **Potential Impact:** This work has the potential to significantly influence the development of PPML techniques for LLMs.  It opens avenues for future research in optimizing FoT and RFA for MPC, exploring other gradient-free optimization methods, and developing more efficient MPC protocols for LLM training. If the community can build upon SECFWT, we might see a surge in secure, collaborative LLM training paradigms.

**Justification of Score:**

Given its novelty in addressing efficient PPFT for LLMs via optimized FoT and RFA for the MPC setting, combined with its clear demonstration of practical benefits, this paper represents a significant contribution. The limitations, particularly around model generalization and performance analysis are noted above.
